• A shallow Western European easing has begun

0 Next week: US and China April activity data; US April CPI

## Trimming the tails

Any forecast is replete with uncertainty and the current tracking of the global expansion is no different. But a focus on potentially large swings in growth and inflation has faded from the scene. Macroeconomic tail risk has been trimmed significantly, and our tracking is shifting to the more mundane elements of global data watching. For the US, we are anticipating a modest rebound in GDP growth to a 2.5%ar this quarter even as soft April business surveys and signs of moderating labor demand have increased downside risks. By contrast the rise in the April allindustry PMI outside the US and lifting in global consumer goods spending to a 3.6%ar last quarter supports our view that the base of global growth is broadening.

Outside the US the growth remains imbalanced as the recent consumer spending lift has been driven entirely by the EM (Figure 1). With spending stalled in Western Europe and Japan and consumer confidence readings still depressed across the DM, sustaining this pickup is by no means assured. On inflation, a 1Q24 acceleration in core CPI gains to a 3.5%ar aligns with our view that the global disinflation process is now stalling. However, last year<sup>’</sup>s experience of frontloading price increases raises questions around our view that core inflation will remain sticky this quarter. Next week, we look for modest April gains in IP and retail sales in both the US and China and another firm US core CPI increase— outcomes that would solidify our forecast for global GDP to rise at a 2.5%ar and core CPI at a 3.2%ar this quarter.

Chair Powell recently noted that there is no significant concern about stagflation— an environment of high unemployment and inflation—a point that we would endorse. The threat of a US recession has sharply receded and more recent concerns

about Western Europe and China have faded. Global inflation remains elevated but does not appear at threat of stepping significantly higher this year. At the other end of the tail, hopes for an immaculate disinflation that quickly returns inflation and interest rates to the pre-pandemic norms looks increasingly unlikely in the face of recent CPI reports and news on labor costs and other input prices.

We have anticipated this year<sup>’</sup>s resilient growth and inflation persistence would narrow the window for central bank easing but the favorable financial market response to changing policy rate expectations has been a surprise. As market expectations of DM policy easing have declined by more than 50bp this year, global equity prices are at record highs (Figure 2). In the US, the Chicago Fed financial conditions index now stands at an easier level than when the FOMC began raising policy rates in early 2022.

A straightforward interpretation of financial market performance is that there is more underlying strength in the global economy than had been anticipated and higher interest rates are reflecting rather than impeding global growth. We lean in this direction as our 2024 growth and policy rate forecasts both move higher. Significantly more weight in our probability tree has been placed on a scenario in which growth is sustained in a high-for-long policy rates environment. However, we have not abandoned our concern that persistent inflation and limited policy ease could gradually undermine the health of the expansion. Here two considerations stand out:

Gradual monetary conditions drag in place. Although broad financial conditions are easy, monetary conditions remain tight and will likely increase vulnerabilities over time. Bank lending standards are still tightening (Figure 3)—even if at a more moderate pace—and elevated borrowing costs will continue to crimp interest-sensitive spending, a development reflected in business and household surveys. Meanwhile private balance sheets are gradually adjusting to higher debt servicing costs. While these costs remain manageable in the aggregate, pressure on more leveraged companies and households is starting to build.

Source: Fed, ECB, BoE, BoJ, J.P. Morgan

Enabling central banks. Although financial markets have reduced expectations on easing this year, markets remain optimistic that significant easing will be forthcoming. By the end of next year policy rates in the US and Western Europe are anticipated to be more than 125bp lower. Behind this optimism stand central banks that guide toward significant easing, while expressing confidence that inflation will return to target and downplaying the risk of further tightening. Central bankers are thus enabling markets to temper the impact of today<sup>’</sup>s elevated policy rate. As such, we see risks of a more turbulent environment if the disinflation narrative told by central banks breaks.

## Not stuck, still sticky

After last quarter<sup>’</sup>s stall in the global disinflation process there is still considerable optimism that core inflation will step down in the current quarter, enabling central banks to push ahead with rate cuts. This optimism is largely linked to expectations that service price inflation will fall from a near-5%ar in 1Q. While expecting some moderation, the alignment of sustained strong service-sector demand and elevated labor costs looks likely to limit this impulse. We also see core goods inflation moving modestly higher. In all, we project the step down in global core CPI to rise 3.2%ar in 2Q24 (Figure 4). The limited April releases so far are broadly consistent with this view as we are tracking a 0.26%m/m gain.

Figure 4: J.P. Morgan global\* core CPI forecast evolution % change saar, forecast by date made
![](images/6a649a425912245594a4191bf10c2b5926d82cce5d65bebeaa9cbbb7d5c47ff3.jpg)

Last quarter<sup>’</sup>s core inflation acceleration was concentrated in the US and Euro area and they should account for the lion<sup>’</sup>s share of this quarter<sup>’</sup>s moderation. The Euro area has already reported a 0.15%m/m gain, with softening more skewed toward core goods than services. Next week<sup>’</sup>s US CPI release is expected to deliver a still firm 0.33%m/m (3.7%oya) gain. In EM, our forecasts assume a material move lower in 2Q core inflation only in LatAm (from 4.2% to 3.3%ar). April releases thus far point to modest upside due to stickier services inflation in Mexico while prints from the CE-3 are validating our view of continued firm core CPI momentum.

## Europe starts on early but shallow easing

Western European central banks are now acting on their guidance and easing rates. Following the SNB cut, the Riksbank lowered its policy rate this week. The ECB April meeting minutes confirmed that a June cut is on track while the Bank of England made a dovish hold this week that opened the door to a possible June ease. While European easing is getting underway, the latest signs show a pickup in growth and sticky inflation for the ECB and BoE. For the Riksbank the case for a substantial easing is stronger and we expect rates to fall a cumulative 125bp this year. However, external pressure on the currency may also slow its path.

The BoE remained on hold this week as expected but did so in a way that raises the chances of a June cut. We have maintained our call for an August start and this week<sup>’</sup>s surprising 2.5%ar 1Q24 GDP gain highlights upside risk to the BoE growth forecast. We expect momentum to remain strong in 2Q, while the BoE anticipates a slowdown to 0.8%ar. This should reduce the urgency to cut alongside upcoming labor market and CPI reports.

The Euro area April PMI confirmed a further pickup in growth and points to continued strong hiring. The implied weakness in productivity that has plagued the region for over a year is concerning policymakers as the resulting rise in unit labor costs adds to inflation pressures. These pressures are being tempered by moderation in wages, however. Negotiated pay growth looks set to ease from a peak of 4.7%oya in 3Q23 to 4.3%oya in 1Q24. At the same time, a deceleration in GDP prices last quarter suggests cost passthrough is moderating. These developments look to have comforted the ECB, whose April meeting minutes reinforced the likelihood of a June cut, even if robust service sector inflation is a key concern that will challenge future cuts.

Despite a weaker exchange rate, a combination of weak growth, softening labor markets, and declining inflation warranted the Riksbank cut this week. External concerns did, however, trigger a more hawkish tone from the board, who closed the door on another cut in June and guided to only two cuts in 2H24. We still look for a next cut in September, with an elevated risk of a move already in August.

## China sugar high set to fade

We expect next week<sup>’</sup>s key activity data for April to show that China<sup>’</sup>s policy- and export-led boom last quarter is fading. After its 7.4%ar surge in 1Q24, we expect China<sup>’</sup>s GDP to expand a muted 4%ar this quarter. Last month<sup>’</sup>s March data set the tone for this downshift, with a contraction in both IP and retail sales. While we look for some firming in next week<sup>’</sup>s April data, we expect it to leave the trajectory soft, with only modest gains in IP and consumer spending. Hous-

ing market activity has remained sluggish in April. The 1Q recovery in consumption in services activity may be losing some momentum, as hinted by the easing in the April services PMI, with alt-data suggesting per-capita tourist spending retreating further during the May labor day holiday period, reflecting lingering softness in household confidence on job security and the income outlook. External demand remains strong, however, as the April trade report shows exports rising 2.8%m/m, with broad-based growth in shipments to DM markets (US and Europe), as well as to ASEAN and other major EM regions (including LatAm and Africa), reinforcing constructive signals on global goods demand. On balance, with the housing market to exert a drag for another one to two years, our forecast assumes 2H24 growth stabilizes at a soft trend pace of 4.3%q/q, saar, with full-year 2024 real GDP growth at 5.2%yoy.

## Markets to EM CBs: Slow your roll

While Western European central banks are starting to ease, EM central bank easing is slowing down. Much of this shift reflects ongoing inflation concerns, but the recent Fed repricing amplifies caution. In EM Asia, currency pressures are most acute amid low-rate buffers. We continue to push back rate cuts across the region and now see the BSP cutting just 50bp this year starting in 4Q24. If the Fed were to remain on hold through next quarter the modest rate cuts we have penciled in elsewhere would likely become untenable despite the region<sup>’</sup>s subpar domestic demand.

For central banks across LatAm and CEE, interest rate buffers are larger, but the pace of easing is slowing and rhetoric is turning more hawkish. In Brazil, the BCB slowed the pace of easing to 25bp amid a strong political division within the board. In Mexico, Banxico<sup>’</sup>s surprisingly hawkish pivot in response to sticky services inflation and the prospect of a more protracted return to the inflation target points to risks that the current pause in the easing cycle could extend into 2H24 (our base case is still for a June cut).

ECB rate cuts should support further modest CE-3 easing in 2H24, but sticky wage growth and recovering domestic demand are promoting caution. Regional retail sales surged last quarter and Hungary and Poland core inflation posted April upside surprises. We now expect both the CNB and NBH to slow the pace of easing to 25bp at their next meetings. In Romania, we keep our call for the NBR to deliver a first 25bp policy rate cut next week but see risks skewed toward no change.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 2Q24 global real GDP growth moved sideways this week at 2.4%ar (Figure 1). Our top-down global nowcaster held at 2.5%ar, suggesting balanced risks to global growth (Table 2).

About 40% of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in 70% of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests a downshift in capex growth from 2.4%ar in 1Q24 to 1.8%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

The April global all-industry PMI points to above-potential 3%ar growth in global GDP. Global business confidence slipped to a slightly more depressed level in April, although a few key manufacturing weak spots saw improvement. A small decline in global consumer confidence last month ended a run of five consecutive gains.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds saw a modest bear flattening over the past week. In the US, the week was light on major data releases, with stronger than expected longer-term inflation expectations in the Michigan survey rising close to recent highs at 3.1%. While valuations look cheap, in the near-term yields are likely somewhat sticky as it will take moderating inflation data to validate dovish Fed expectations. We stay neutral duration ahead of inflation data and look to add bullish front-end exposure on further yield rises, and keep 5s/30s steepeners to position for eventual easing and higher term premia.

In the Euro area, the minutes of the April meeting reinforced the sense that the ECB is set for a June cut. Over the medium term, we retain a bullish stance given an approaching start to a cutting cycle and gradual easing thereafter, but the lack of near-term catalysts keeps us neutral for now. We keep steepeners via 10s/15s Germany and 2Yx2Y/10Yx10Y EUR swaps. In the UK, the BoE provided a modestly dovish tilt despite stronger data; we keep fronts/reds SONIA curve flatteners as a positive carry bullish proxy.

In EM, near-term growth prospects remain solid, but the disinflation process has slowed and become more varied across regions. While cautious EM policy rate cuts continue, the scope for cuts in 2H24 has diminished, particularly in Asia. We stay neutral local duration and hold selective OWs in Brazil, Colombia, Uruguay, Czechia and Poland, offset by a short duration overlay and an UW in Thailand (EMOS, May 8<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

US HG bond spreads have been trading in a steady range of 100-107bps for the past two months. Recently, they closed at the tighter end of this range, while HG bond yields have drifted lower post the more benign than expected Fed commentary and Payroll report last week. In this quiet week for macro and earnings data the true picture of HG bond technicals was apparent. Spreads rallied despite a surge in issuance, with \$51bn of supply in the first three days of the week, marking the largest weekly supply tally since mid-February 2024. This indicates the robust technicals in the US HG bond market, with demand remaining strong even in the face of increased supply (Credit Market Outlook & Strategy, May 10<sup>th</sup>). FOMC meeting outcome a clear negative for most foreign buyers, as long-end USD yields fell significantly while hedging costs rose slightly with the prospect of less or delayed convergence between the Fed and other central banks. As a result, the JPM Foreign Attractiveness of USD IG Bonds index fell sharply to its lowest level since Feb 2024 (HG Credit Foreign Demand Monitor, May 6<sup>th</sup>).

Our new monthly Spread Strategy Spotlight publication looks at credit and broader spread markets from the topdown. The first edition comes against the backdrop of the April US payroll and employment reports. Judged from the perspective of not-being-too-hot-nor-being-too-cold, the data could not have been better for spread and broader risk markets. We are not complacent but do not feel overly bearish with all-in yields remaining a powerful offset to optically thin spreads (Spread Strategy Spotlight, May 6<sup>th</sup>).

We expect euro IG to continue to trade range bound over the near-term. In our view, we are currently in a <sup>‘</sup>bad news is good news<sup>’</sup> trading environment, with the recent modest softening in the US data reducing the risk of a renewed policy hiking cycle. Despite perceptions that HY gap risk has increased, the proportion of bonds falling >10pts in a month was just 0.6% of the market in 1Q, compared with 0.9% which rose >10pts (European Credit Weekly, May 10<sup>th</sup>).

Source: J.P. Morgan.

## Currencies

High-for-long would trump fading US exceptionalism. The medium-term view is still bullish USD on high yields, growth cushion and other supports, but tactical concerns stem from nascent signs of fading US growth exceptionalism and saturated investor longs. Inflation divergences will be key as central banks are inflation- rather than growth-focused. The upcoming US CPI will set the tone for FX as Fed pricing has been the single biggest driver of the dollar. Implications from a soft/firm US inflation print are obvious for the direction of

Fed pricing, but more nuanced for USD. DXY has been more sensitive to inflation misses. Having delivered solid 6% 1Q returns, returns from global carry flat-lined in April; returns from G10 carry have dominated EM YTD. Stay bullish USD vs. EUR, GBP, CNH and CHF, primarily via options, partially hedged with NOK vs. EUR and CAD. In EM, neutral overall with regional differentiation (overweight Latam and EMEA, underweight Asia).

FX forecasts are mostly unchanged: G10 is unchanged ex-Scandis. EUR/USD 1.05 (3Q); USD/JPY 154, but with upside risks on BoJ dovishness. EUR/SEK 1Q<sup>’</sup>25 10.90 (10.30). EM: USD/CNY 3Q 7.30. USD/MXN 3Q 17.50; USD/BRL 5.05. USD/ZAR 2Q 18.80 (19.00) upgraded on reduced election tail risks. EUR/CZK 3Q 25.75 (26.00) upgraded on hawkish CNB despite bearish outlook (KCV, May 10<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

US Election Implications for Commodities. There could be little difference in overall macro direction as extreme proposals are unlikely to materialize given the high probability of a split Congress. Among major differences, a change in the administration would mean 1) A possible major realignment of federal energy policy, which would likely not result in higher US oil and gas output but would lighten the regulatory burden on oil and gas companies. 2) The US foreign policy on Ukraine, Iran, Iraq, and Venezuela could also see significant changes under Trump. 3) The potential for a re-escalating tariff war, particularly between China and the US, which is a major bearish risk for base metals via resulting FX and growth consequences. 4) For agri markets the prospect of a firmer dollar and potential trade tensions comes at an inconvenient time when the US is well stocked with a large export surplus of grain and oilseeds. Under Trump, it is unlikely that energy components of Biden<sup>’</sup>s IRA law would be materially altered since a majority of investments is destined for Republican states/districts, but consumer elements (eg. incentives for EVs and home heating) could be scaled back (Global Commodities, May 7<sup>th</sup>).

Türkiye - with rising domestic production and diversified import sources - is playing a growing role in the European Natural Gas market. We expect Turkish domestic production to increase, theoretically freeing up LNG supply for delivery to Europe and Asia after the Ukraine/Russia transit deal ends With ample import capacity and potential enhancements in export capacities, Türkiye could export/transit up to 40 Bcm/year of natural gas to Europe by 2030 – a 60% increase from 2023 levels (European Natural Gas, May 8<sup>th</sup>).

Globally, oil demand grew by 1.9 mbd y/y, above our expectations of a 1.7 mbd increase. India<sup>’</sup>s April total oil demand is at a seasonal all-time high. Diesel cracks in the US and Europe rose 4-6% w/w as Russian diesel exports struggle to improve. In China, oil demand is below our estimates due to lower industrial demand on account of the Labor Day holiday. Global flight activity is the highest since the pandemic (Oil Demand Tracker, May 8<sup>th</sup>). April commodity availability edges up on US gas, despite broader sectoral declines. On an Ex-China basis, base metals and agri commodities availability declined, while energy availability increased. Commodity inventories typically expand through April on a seasonal basis, but the build in availability this year was wellbelow the seasonal norm (Global Commodities, May 7<sup>th</sup>).

## Japan: BoJ to choose the lesser of two evils

Trend yen weakness is putting pressure on the BoJ, as dissatisfaction among households is on the rise

But if forced to choose, the BoJ likely will allow yen weakening, rather than rapid rate hikes...

• ...as the weak yen still benefits the economy through higher corporate revenue

Balancing with risks of capital outflows, we expect the BoJ to deliver rate hikes more than recently hinted

The BoJ is facing the rates versus fx tradeoff again. Continued downward pressure on the yen has led to rising dissatisfaction among domestic consumers, as it undermines their purchasing power. The MoF continues to control the pace of yen depreciation through fx intervention, but it is becoming apparent that it can only slow yen declines temporarily. In Japan, calls for a policy response to curb yen depreciation are rising, arguing that further weakening of the yen is damaging the economy.

Contrary to rate hikes that are expected to drag the overall economy, the impact of a weaker yen on the Japanese economy is not necessarily negative. With exporters<sup>’</sup> reorientation of production bases over the past decade, export volumes have barely increased even with the weaker yen. But the weak yen has boosted corporate earnings, which are staying around record highs. Those gains have not been sufficiently distributed to workers: firms are delivering decades-high wage growth but remain cautious on labor costs given the constraints of a rigid labor market with persistent expectations of lifetime employment. The flip side of this is persistent real income drags. Positive real wage growth has yet to come into sight, even after two consecutive years of drags. The weaker yen certainly brought benefits to the economy, but this has not yet led to a rise in overall domestic demand due to the clogging of the corporate-to-household income channel.

Against this backdrop, the BoJ remains cautious about future rate hikes. Governor Ueda<sup>’</sup>s remarks after the April policy meeting point toward hikes about once every six months, bringing the policy rate above 1.5% by mid-2026. However, the underlying pressure on the yen also reveals the risks this phased approach could pose. Rapid weakening of the yen could potentially undermine confidence in the yen at home, which in itself may raise the risk of further weakening of the yen through a rise in capital outflows. Balancing these risks, we expect the BoJ to raise policy rates at a faster pace than hinted, to 50bp by end-2024.

## The real problem is not the weak yen

The key benefits of the weak currency for the economy are on exports and the boost to corporate earnings. With the relatively large presence of the manufacturing sector in the economy, Japan<sup>’</sup>s policy settings have tended to prefer a weaker yen. The benefits of a weaker yen on exports and corporate earnings for the economy as a whole have been thought to outweigh the negative impact of higher import prices. These benefits have yet to become so apparent this time around, particularly for exports. Despite the significant weakening of the yen over the past couple of years, Japan<sup>’</sup>s export volume has remained largely flat (Figure 1). This may be partly attributable to weak global goods demand in the post-pandemic recovery, but more importantly it reflects a structural shift in Japan<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing sector.

Figure 1: Real exports and manufacturers' profits
![](images/3845f146c5a21bc34be03c8b41bdbe0a8f904bc064b8d013a40e19e78dd8f172.jpg)

With decades-long manufacturing globalization and the stronger yen after the Global Financial Crisis, Japan<sup>’</sup>s manufacturers have increased their overseas production ratios over the past 20 years; as of 2023, close to 40% of both manufacturing production and sales were from overseas. This has led to a decline in Japan<sup>’</sup>s export sensitivity to a weaker yen, structurally limiting the benefits of a weaker yen for exports.

That said, the weaker yen has led to a significant improvement in manufacturers<sup>’</sup> profits. This has been clear after 2022 as the yen<sup>’</sup>s weakening trend has proceeded. Since 2022, yendenominated corporate profits have stayed at record-high levels, despite export volumes staying flat and dollar-denominated corporate profits having moderated after the initial postpandemic surge.

The benefits of the weak yen are not just for manufacturers. Despite weak domestic consumption demand, nonmanufacturers continue to perform well in terms of both corporate profits and business sentiment (Figures 2, 3). This may have been partly attributable to post-pandemic carryover demand and increased margins through the government initiative to encourage firms to raise cost pass-through, but also to a sharp increase in inbound consumption driven by a weaker yen.

Figure 2: Current profit, nonmanufacturers
![](images/7eae7fdaf3ab5d663c87462d3bf015bd6ccc7a832e755eafdccffbcef8de6c87.jpg)

Figure 3: Nonmanufacturers' business sentiment
![](images/1c48199059d0c6c2f7e52c8870d5ab580fc81e7b5955ebb7078d84beefe316ed.jpg)

In fact, the number of foreign visitors to Japan has recovered rapidly since late 2022, and by March 2024, it had exceeded the record of 3 million. And the weak yen is acting in the direction of increasing inbound consumption. Although consumption per foreign visitor remained largely flat compared with the pre-pandemic average in US dollar terms, that in JPY terms increased by about 20% due to the weak yen (Figure 4). Although this should account for only about 1.5% of GDP, these factors are driving up nonmanufacturers<sup>’</sup> business sentiment and profits, contrary to sluggish domestic consumption demand.

Figure 4: Foreign visitor arrivals and their spending
![](images/f3b7da6ebbade9d108c768fda443967ced72e944423502e47bdbbc851157eb5d.jpg)

Strong corporate earnings boosted by the weaker yen have led to higher wages. Indeed, firms have provided decades-high

wage increases for two consecutive years in FY2023 and FY2024. While structurally tight labor market conditions were also behind this, such large and broad-based wage increases would not have been possible without strong corporate earnings. Although the benefits of the weaker yen for the Japanese economy are likely to have declined compared to the past due to structural changes in export industries, they still have large positive impacts on the economy.

## Something that causes clogging

Nevertheless, domestic dissatisfaction with yen weakening is growing. The main concern is downward pressure on real domestic consumption caused by higher import prices. Japan<sup>’</sup>s domestic consumption has trended down since 2Q23, after an initial post-pandemic recovery in 1Q23 (Figure 5). This is due to the two consecutive years of real income drags for households, as nominal wage growth has failed to catch up with inflation. More recently, prospects for fading the real income drags have risen, reflecting the strong results of the FY2024 spring wage negotiations. But if the yen continues to weaken, risks of a further decline in real wages will increase.

Figure 5: Real consumption and labor income 2016=100, sa, 3mma
![](images/515a37e7ac992bc598bb0b5c10a3b380b65e3800ee0c4f89076ba1c66b09096e.jpg)

The limited benefits to the economy as a whole despite the apparent tailwind of a weaker yen for corporate earnings are likely attributable to conservative corporate wage-setting behavior amid a rigid labor market. Firms have indeed raised wages since 2023, backed by strong corporate revenues and rising inflation expectations. But this has yet to reach a level that offsets inflation, leaving real wage growth negative (Figure 6). This is also evident in firms<sup>’</sup> labor distribution ratios (Figure 7). The labor distribution ratio has declined since mid-2022, with the all-industry average more than 5%-pts below the 2000-2023 average by end-2023. This is even more pronounced with large firms but is no exception for SMEs, which are generally considered to have limited resources to raise wages. Firms appear reluctant to raise labor costs, even as the labor market structurally tightens. This is apparently a clog in the system, preventing more positive spillovers of the weaker yen to the overall economy.

Figure 6: Wages and their determinant factors
![](images/abeb97e074f1d28600f6371ffd123e9d7e46ea79f0bdc178fb483c7254d8f7ed.jpg)
Source: MoF, Statistics Bureau, MHLW, J.P. Morgan

Figure 7: Labor distribution ratios by scale of firms
![](images/891a7eb145c52647c7ea67d0781e9e314b80eed0f23673a66b3a8e6b3c8f6177.jpg)

We believe this clogging is caused by a rigid labor market structure. In Japan, even with real labor income falling for two consecutive years, the amount of job turnover prompted by a search for higher wages has not increased markedly, reflecting a persistent lifetime employment mindset. This mindset also requires firms to retain employment even in the event of lower revenue due to changes in the economic environment. Thus, firms need to be cautious about raising labor costs to balance with future risks of lower profits. As a result, firms tend to retain sufficient revenues while limiting wage increases, even when they have capacity to raise wages. The threat to real labor income is likely not a weaker yen itself, but a rigid labor market structure that has prevented the benefits of the weaker yen from reaching households.

## BoJ to balance the risks

The BoJ is facing a rates-versus-fx tradeoff. MoF currency intervention is becoming less effective in limiting yen declines. Hence, raising policy rates looks inevitable to reverse the weak yen trend. However, rate hikes will increase the government<sup>’</sup>s financial burden and curb domestic demand, which remains anemic. While the weak yen is not necessarily damaging the economy, rate hikes clearly will restrain the economy. The incentive for the BoJ would be to allow some currency depreciation rather than delivering rapid rate hikes, particularly given the current weak state of domestic demand. This is also evident in Governor Ueda<sup>’</sup>s remarks: asked about the economic impact of the weak yen, he said that “some of the effects (of the weak yen) on the demand side are positive,<sup>”</sup> while “interest rate hikes will adversely affect a wide range of economic sectors.<sup>”</sup> The BoJ still seems to think that rising interest rates will have a greater negative impact on Japan<sup>’</sup>s overall economy than the weak currency, and we agree.

The BoJ<sup>’</sup>s caution on rate hikes was revealed at the April post-meeting press conference. Governor Ueda noted “the prospect of the policy rate reaching around neutral sometime in the second half of the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s forecasts period (given the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s forecasts horizon through FY2026, this means sometime between 4Q25 and 1Q27)<sup>”</sup>. And the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s estimates of r\* appear to be somewhere between -1% and +0.5%. This means the BoJ currently expects to deliver rate hikes about once every six months, bringing the policy rate to above 1.5% by mid-2026 (Figure 8).

Figure 8: BoJ's hinted policy rate path
![](images/b40967ceaf90a2d37b579275fc8c9f27fb87c1c7fcac1551797c00be224920ad.jpg)

The BoJ<sup>’</sup>s phased approach could cause further weakening of the yen. We have long argued that the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s cautious stance on policy normalization amid the rapid changes in inflation dynamics involves risks of stimulating capital outflows. This risk is rising, as the recent steady decline in the yen could lead to a loss of households<sup>’</sup> confidence in the currency.

The government and BoJ appear to be aware of the risk. At the press conference after the April policy meeting, Governor Ueda maintained his suggestion of phased rate hikes based on the possible changes in underlying inflation. But he also said “the monetary policy could change if changes in the currency have a non-negligible impact on inflation prospects.<sup>”</sup> We think it is unusual for the BoJ Governor to suggest a policy reaction to fx fluctuations. While the weak yen is desirable for the BoJ to achieve its policy goals, the BoJ may also need to control the pace of yen depreciation.

Our forecast remains that the weak yen will bring forward the policy rate hikes. The BoJ likely will prefer avoiding consecutive rate hikes unless the yen plunges in a disorderly manner, but it will still not ignore the risk of rising inflation expectations and losing domestic confidence due to the continued weakening of the yen. We continue to expect the BoJ to raise the policy rate to 50bp by end-2024. However, as the global economy appears to remain resilient, yen weakness is more likely to make Japan<sup>’</sup>s inflation sticky. So the risks surrounding the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s policy rate forecasts are, in our view, clearly biased upward.

## West Africa: Cocoa is the new oil

• Cocoa prices have recorded an astronomical rise this year following supply side driven shocks

• West Africa accounts for c.80% of the world<sup>’</sup>s supply with Ghana and Cote d’Ivoire at just over 60%

• Price surge will support FX buffers, but impact limited by forward commitments

• Supply side and output challenges will also have some pass-through to economic growth

Cocoa prices have exploded through 2024 to nominal historical highs, recently towards US\$12,000/tonne (basis prompt NY futures). This is up almost threefold YTD following acute production shortfalls in West Africa and amid resilient demand. These significantly higher prices should positively impact external balances for exporters. However, the top producers in West Africa (Cote d<sup>’</sup>Ivoire (CIV) and Ghana) have mostly sold their output forward for the current season. We expect the impact on their trade balances will be more meaningful from the 2024/2025 season. The persistent decline in cocoa output will also have some growth implications; agriculture typically accounts for about 20-30% of these economies, with cocoa output being dominant within that share.

Fixing the largest-ever physical deficit will be a multi-year hurdle, but remunerative farm-gate prices for farmers across the region will be a helpful start. This should incentivise increased investment into the sector from the farmers who have also faced input pressures from higher fertiliser prices recently. Meanwhile, we expect that cocoa prices will remain structurally higher over the medium term to limit demand while farmers reinvest in tree stock.

## Largest ever supply deficit in Cocoa market

The parabolic rally in world cocoa prices through early 2024 has deep structural roots on the supply side: the market entered its largest ever deficit in 2023/2024. In 2023, cocoa prices rallied over 60% as the global supply and demand balance continued to tighten and world stocks dropped to a sixyear low. The subsequent surge in 2024 has been fuelled by commercial participants rolling physical contracts forward to the 2024/25 season due to an inability to honour commitments for 2023/24. Investor participation has also contributed, which peaked in late February at 60.5% market share, and more recently, cocoa consumers have been scrambling to add forward physical and price cover amid thin market liquidity.

Figure 1: Cocoa nominal and real prices
![](images/fe51957424241673ba9b6906dba6b86c724fcf8e5969b08ed38a39ff618c32f6.jpg)
Source: ICE, Bloomberg Finance L.P, J.P. Morgan

West African origins typically supply around 80% of the world market. After an intense wet season, and strong Harmattan in 2023, 2023/24 season production in world leader Cote d<sup>’</sup>Ivoire is projected to be down 20% YoY to an eightyear low (of 1.8m tonnes). In Ghana, production is projected to be down 11% YoY to a multi decade low of 580,000 tonnes. The International Cocoa Organization estimates the physical deficit in the current season could top 374,000 tonnes . More concerning, 2023/24 marks the third season of a deficit balance – where carryover inventory is drawn on to satiate demand. As a result the stocks-to-grindings ratio has collapsed to the lowest level of forward cover on record of an estimated 29% (relative to the 10-year average of 38%).

Figure 2: World Cocoa market supply and demand balance
![](images/ab0d358d8cf850a7a6a07578b2bb782b8579da5d7a23cc3d2568c572ce1f9e29.jpg)
Source: ICCO, J.P. Morgan

## Trade balances could get a boost...

We estimate that a 10% rise in cocoa prices could lead to a 1.8%pt of GDP rise in CIV<sup>’</sup>s trade balance and 0.7%pt in Ghana (Figure 3). The largest impact will be for CIV, which is the world<sup>’</sup>s largest cocoa producer and we have assumed production flat at the recent low of c.1.8mn tonnes. However, given the structure of the cocoa market where the West African producers mostly engage in forward sales- six months for CIV and longer for Ghana, both top producers are unlikely to see the price surge feed through into significantly higher trade balances this season.

Figure 3: Trade balance sensitivity % of GDP, impact of a 10% rise in cocoa prices
![](images/ee16e4591defceef3ec94cf33a8cfa1f9d18f890cd94974001778e4a2530a8cb.jpg)

Cameroon is the fourth-largest cocoa producer in the world and a 10% rise in prices should see its trade balance improve by 0.6%pt of GDP. While Nigeria ranks amongst the top world producers, the impact of higher cocoa prices on its trade balance is negligible owing to its heavy reliance on oil, which accounts for c.90% of total exports. Against this backdrop, we expect Ghana<sup>’</sup>s current account surplus at only 1% of GDP this year before rising to 1.4% of GDP in 2025, while the IMF projects CIV<sup>’</sup>s current account deficit will narrow by about 4%pts over the course of the next two years

## … while growth is biased to the downside

Cocoa is the biggest driver of agricultural output in CIV and Ghana, and the continued projected decline in cocoa output could be consequential for economic growth. In Ghana, the contribution to GDP growth from the cocoa sector has averaged around 30% in the last eight quarters. On a seasonallyadjusted basis, cocoa GDP accounted for 0.27%pts from the total 0.96%pt recorded in 4Q23 (Figure 4). This comes despite the persistent decline in cocoa output in recent years. Ghana<sup>’</sup>s cocoa marketing board (Cocobod) typically engages in annual syndication loan to finance bean purchases from farmers. Last year, it was only able to secure US\$800mn (the lowest in about a decade) and that is asymptomatic of the output challenges the country is facing.

Figure 4: Ghana contribution to GDP
![](images/0db0b04fd58246167825ddd99171e1e853ce0acedac1f2cd101893238c5f245d.jpg)
Source: Haver, Ghana Statistical Service, J.P. Morgan

In CIV, only 0.2% of the country<sup>’</sup>s cropland is under irrigation, which makes it more vulnerable to adverse weather and climate change. The IMF projects that agricultural productivity in CIV has declined by 35% due to climate change. The World Bank also estimates that land suitable for cocoa production could shrink by about half by 2050.

Table 1: Cocoa Output
<table><tr><td>Mn tonnes</td><td>5y average</td><td>22/23</td><td>23/24</td></tr><tr><td>Cote d&#x27;Ivoire</td><td>2.20</td><td>2.30</td><td>1.75</td></tr><tr><td>Ghana</td><td>0.65</td><td>0.75</td><td>0.58</td></tr><tr><td>Cameroon</td><td>0.30</td><td>0.29</td><td>0.30</td></tr><tr><td>Nigeria</td><td>0.28</td><td>0.29</td><td>0.27</td></tr></table>

Source: ICCO, CAN, Cocoa Board, Bloomberg L.P. Finance, J.P. Morgan

## Cocoa prices could be higher for longer

We expect that cocoa prices will remain structurally higher over the medium term, to limit demand while farmers eradicate disease and reinvest in tree stock. While further shortterm hikes should not be ruled out, over the medium term when the deficit eases, we look for prices to decline to around the \$6,000 mark (basis prompt NY futures). This could possibly materialise as the ongoing El Niño phenomenon (the warming of sea surface temperatures) is gradually weakening, and a transition to La Niña (the cooling of sea surface temperatures) in the summer is likely. Under this scenario, weather threats for cocoa production ease somewhat and yields may improve under better rainfall across West Africa and Asia. This could be supportive of main crop output in the 2024/25 season for over 80% of the world<sup>’</sup>s cocoa-producing countries

## Australian consumers: deeper pockets

• Real consumption growth flat-lined in 2023 owing to policy tightening and negative real wage growth

• The marginal drag from rate hikes is becoming less intense, opening the door to a consumption recovery

• Tax cuts and rising real wage growth will also support spending

Australian household real consumption growth has slowed considerably in recent years given tighter financial conditions and deeply negative real wage growth. The surge in net migration means per capita consumption has been materially weaker than the aggregate readings, contracting for the past five consecutive quarters and in level terms undershooting the long term trend (Figure 1). While uncertainty is elevated and headwinds remain, we think real consumer spending (and so GDP) growth is poised to pick up as policy and income dynamics turn more favorable.

Our forecast expects real household consumption to average 0.2%q/q in 1H24 before accelerating toward 0.5%q/q later in the year, still soft in historical terms but a meaningful step-up from last year<sup>’</sup>s average quarterly run rate of around zero. Importantly, there is space for this pick-up to occur without pushing growth back above potential, mitigating the need for a monetary policy response. The RBA<sup>’</sup>s forecasts also imply an acceleration in consumer spending in 2024 with the slated improvement, in our view, consistent with the cash rate on hold this year, before modest easing in early 2025.

Figure 1: Real household consumption
![](images/3402a987a7a1e2fd6464304642306532faea415c0b43503c3277215ca361bb23.jpg)

## Tracking transmission

Monetary policy influences the economy through numerous channels including household disposable income, savings/ investment behavior, the exchange rate, and asset prices. The RBA has previously noted that the pass-through of policy changes occurs relatively quickly in Australia versus global peers, largely owing to the floating rate structure of the mortgage market. We have previously estimated the impulse from cash rate changes to real GDP and found that based on the speed, magnitude and empirical lag structure the full effect of tighter financial conditions becomes evident in the activity data four to six quarters following the policy change. Given the RBA delivered the majority of the tightening in 2022/early 2023, most of the policy-induced drag on real GDP is already evident in the data (Figure 2). Policy transmission is difficult to gauge and the prevalence of fixed rate mortgage flows between 2020 and 2022 may have elongated the cycle a little. But with majority of these resets landing in 2H23 we argue most of the impulse has now been realized, and that the marginal drag on activity will dissipate from the current pace.

Figure 2: RBA cash rate and impact on GDP
![](images/a57be5fc3fbf58b864198f080bfa3d79be9eedfebb8b9879e942e84ffc6ba183.jpg)

The household debt servicing ratio tells a similar story. Following a rapid adjustment from early 2022 the scheduled mortgage payment (principal + interest) to income ratio reported a trough to peak increase of \~280bps with this rate now tracking a shade under 10%.

Figure 3: Mortgage repayment to income ratio
![](images/3c765b9d4e8050388fb8b3eaa294d70a09d43699dbe9965381a608155a2a8220.jpg)

However, as shown in Figure 3 the rate of increase is slowing and indicates the marginal drag on disposable income growth is moderating. Levels also matter, and mortgage rates are at the highest level in more than a decade, so we don<sup>’</sup>t see a lot of space for an acceleration in nominal spending. But, given our forecast that the cash rate has peaked and the next move is likely to be a cut (1Q25), we expect debt servicing dynamics to be less of a drag, allowing consumption to grow more in line with nominal incomes from here.

## Slightly watered down tax policy

Earlier this year we noted that the implementation of stage three of the personal income tax cuts would provide a modest boost to household spending after accounting for the distribution of tax changes, and each income groups<sup>’</sup> marginal propensity to consume (MPC). Since then the government has reworked the composition of legislated cuts, which now have a more equitable distribution across the income range with larger tax breaks for the lower income quintiles and smaller reductions for higher income individuals (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Tax benefit accruing to income groups % of total tax savings accruing to groups
![](images/64d8b3985c9badaf087686e3b8e15dffa8a65813cbc8ac963423872c64d5274a.jpg)

Applying the same analysis from earlier in the year we find tax cuts should lift aggregate spending by up to A\$12.5 billion (around 0.5% of GDP). As before, tax changes will lower Pay As You Go (PAYG) collections through the year and increase after-tax income each pay cycle from July 2024, meaning the impulse to aggregate spending will be smoothed over time and potentially difficult to detect. With that in mind, households should also be somewhat forward-looking so it is possible that the retail data, for example, get a bump from around mid-year as households adjust to higher after-tax income. Inflation implications should be relatively minimal with a consumption boost of this size empirically associated with a 10bp boost to headline inflation.

## Real improvement

Like most DM economies, Australia<sup>’</sup>s labor market has proven resilient in the face of rapid policy tightening. The jobless rate is only three tenths above the post-COVID low at 3.8%, and (different to many other DMs) more than a full percentage point below the pre-COVID jumping off point. Labor market tightness has had the expected effect on wage growth, which broadly traced the (wage) Phillips curve higher to 4%oya, the strongest outcome in the post 2008/09 era. That said, this acceleration paled in comparison to inflation, resulting in deeply negative real wage growth despite the jobless rate plumbing multi-decade lows (Figure 5).

Wages are empirically stickier than prices, though Australia is a particularly special case given the prevalence of long term employment contracts has resulted in a slow(er) moving wage cycle which is less sensitive to labor supply/demand dynamics. We have previously explored these dynamics in depth, but in summary enterprise bargaining/collective agreements comprise about 35% of all wage contracts, whereas the legislated award/minimum wages dictates conditions for a further 20%. The average term of an enterprise bargaining agreement (EBA) is 36 months, while legislated wage outcomes only reset annually, meaning it takes time for underlying labor conditions to be marked through to the aggregate wage data, trimming peaks/troughs in wage growth through the cycle. The impact of these lags is evident in the recent data, with many newly-struck EBAs delivering wage outcomes above the prevailing WPI. But with less than 10% of these contracts expiring in an average quarter, the impulse to aggregate wage growth is relatively small.

Figure 5: Real wages and unemployment %oya, real wage growth
![](images/75b276e09762db4260cccedb883ec395a22eabae85b7ece5956a968521474d70.jpg)

The elongated nature of the wage cycle means aggregate nominal wage growth is likely to hold around current levels in upcoming quarters, even as unemployment increases, and inflation comes down. In our view the stickiness of wages relative to inflation will deliver a meaningful improvement in real wage growth, which we expect to traverse a 0.5%oya - 1% range this year and next, having been significantly negative in recent years. Of course, this won<sup>’</sup>t happen in a vacuum, and the feedback loop to labor demand will bear monitoring.

We have highlighted the challenges that wage lags present to corporate profit margins at this time of the cycle. Though downside to margins mostly reflects normalization from unusually high levels, and the pace of real wage growth we expect is only marginally stronger than the historical average. As such we don<sup>’</sup>t anticipate a particularly disruptive adjustment in hiring at this stage. The combination of fading marginal drags on disposable income from mortgage rates, the implementation of tax cuts, and rising real wage growth should then generate a modest recovery in real household consumption through 2024 even with unemployment trending a little higher.

# Hong Kong’s prospects as a financial center

HK SAR is a leading offshore RMB business hub, with the largest CNH liquidity pool

Financial linkages with the Mainland have been deepening, with the family of Connect schemes expanding

As headwinds hit the equity market, AWM business, etc., there have been concerns on HK’s financial sector

• The major cyclical factor is the high interest rate environment

Structural factors include mainland China’s deflation pressure and slowing potential growth, as well as geopolitical tensions

The China Securities Regulatory Commission (CSRC) announced five measures on capital market cooperation with HK SAR on April 19<sup>th</sup> (link in Chinese), aiming at further enhancing the Stock Connect and supporting the city's strengthening of its status as an international financial center. New measures include expanding the scope of eligible ETFs under the Stock Connect; incorporating REITs into the Stock Connect; supporting the inclusion of RMB-denominated stocks into southbound Stock Connect; optimizing the mutual recognition of funds; and supporting the listing of leading mainland companies in HK SAR.

This announcement comes against the backdrop of deepening financial linkages between HK SAR and the mainland in the past decade, and rising concerns over HK<sup>’</sup>s financial sector and its status as an international financial center (IFC) in recent years. On the one hand, HK SAR has been a leading offshore RMB business hub, holding the largest offshore RMB liquidity pool, and handles the most RMB trade settlements outside of the Mainland. It also originates and intermediates two-thirds of mainland China<sup>’</sup>s inward foreign direct investment and outward direct investment (link). Meanwhile, various Connect schemes have been built (Figure 1), facilitating the cross-border trade of stocks, bonds and other assets between HK SAR and mainland China, and more importantly, the two-way capital flow between mainland China and the rest or the world. On the other hand, the weak performance of the equity market in recent years, headwinds towards the asset and wealth management (AWM) business, and the relocation of multinational companies have raised concerns on HK<sup>’</sup>s status as an international financial center (IFC).

Both cyclical and structural forces are at work. Current high interest rates are the major cyclical factor, which may last longer than previously expected. The structural challenges include China<sup>’</sup>s deflation pressure on corporate earnings,

slowing potential growth in China, and geopolitical tensions. These may have prolonged effects. In our view, it is in HK SAR<sup>’</sup>s interest to further deepen its financial linkages with the Mainland.

Figure 1: Access channels between HK SAR and mainland China
![](images/238ae55649fa19080f7eccd9e5b35cc3d14f7077f59198f7fa0ffb01fdbdad2c.jpg)
Source: HKMA

## Leading offshore RMB business hub

Why is HK SAR so important to China? From an economic financial point of view, one of the top reasons is that HK is the leading offshore RMB business hub. It holds the largest offshore RMB liquidity pool (Figure 2). In December 2023, RMB deposits in HK reached 939.2 bn yuan, rising 12.4%oya. Meanwhile, HK handles more than 70% of global RMB SWIFT payments. The RMB trade settlement handled by banks in Hong Kong reached 11.7 trillion yuan in 2023, rising 24.9%y/y. HK plays an irreplaceable role in RMB internationalization.

Figure 2: Offshore RMB deposits
![](images/b9e4a2e6e57d1d668b0065d0a095a79f5f3a2007c8c2ef54075e7bf292268ad3.jpg)

Hong Kong is also the largest offshore RMB bond market. In 2023, offshore RMB bond issuance in HK was 334.5 bn yuan, more than doubled compared with 2022. In order for offshore investors to directly participate in China<sup>’</sup>s onshore bond market, the Northbound Bond Connect scheme was launched in July 2017. Bonds are held through the nominee accounts opened by the Central Moneymarkets Unit (CMU) in the Mainland with Shanghai Clearing House (SHCH) and China Central Depository & Clearing (CCDC), and no onshore presence is required for investors. In 2023, Northbound trading turnover volume reached 9.97 bn yuan, more than ten times

the size in 2018. In September 2021, Southbound Bond Connect was launched, allowing eligible onshore investors to invest in eligible bond and trade directly with market makers in HK. In May 2023, Northbound Trading of Swap Connect was launched to provide a convenient and secure channel for international investors to trade interest rate swap products on the Mainland via a connection between infrastructure institutions on the Mainland and in HK.

## Recent weaknesses in equity market

The stock market in HK has been an important source of funds for Chinese companies, thanks to its advantages in market regulation and financial innovation. HKEX data suggests that mainland Chinese companies accounted for 77.5% of the total market capitalization as of March 2024. This also suggest that HK<sup>’</sup>s stock market is strongly related with mainland China<sup>’</sup>s economic conditions.

However, the HK<sup>’</sup>s equity market faced headwinds in the past two years. The Hang Seng Index has fallen from the peak in February 2021 at 31,084 to below 16,000 in Jan-Feb 2024, amid unfavorable interest rate environment and weak economic sentiment. Primary market activities also slowed (Figure 3). Funds raised through HK IPOs in 2023 were HK\$46.3bn, only 11.6% of the 2020 level, while the number of IPOs in 2023 was 47.4% of the 2020 level.

Figure 3: IPO market in Hong Kong
![](images/0cc735697b18a9c9b68551bd74afc3879e6bebae6a1d4dc3c262ec3b87568599.jpg)
Source: Hong Kong Exchanges and Clearing Limited

Nevertheless, stock connect schemes still registered steady net inflows into HK SAR. The cumulative net buying through Southbound Stock Connect amounted to HK\$2,890bn (\~US\$370bn) by end-2023, rising HK\$318.8bn (\~US\$41bn) from end-2022. Net outflows through Northbound Stock Connect fell significantly in 2023 compared with two years ago, and the net inflow through the stock connect schemes into HK in 2023 was US\$33.0bn.

From mainland China<sup>’</sup>s perspective, the Northbound Stock Connect itself has become a more important channel than Qualified Foreign Institutional Investor (QFII) scheme to invest in mainland China. Note that the cumulative approved

QFII amounted to US\$116.3bn as of May 2020, while the cumulative net buying through Northbound Stock Connect was \~US\$150 billion at the same time, which has increased to \~US\$250 billion in April 2024.

## Asset and wealth management business

Hong Kong is also an international asset and wealth management (AWM) business center. According to the SFC, the total AUM of the AWM business amounted to HK\$34.7 trillion (or \~12.1x the nominal GDP) in 2021, yet fell to HK\$29.8 trillion in 2022 (Figure 4).

The investor base is diversified globally. Assets from non-HK SAR investors represented 64% of the AUM in 2022, with 23% from North America, 14% from rest of APAC, 11% from Europe and UK, and 9% from mainland China. About a half of the investments are allocated to mainland China and HK SAR. For the asset management and fund advisory business (by licensed corporations, registered institutions, and insurance companies), 23% of the HK\$11.4 trillion AUM in 2022 are allocated in HK SAR, and 22% are allocated in mainland China, followed by 16% in North America and 14% in Europe and the UK.

Figure 4: HK asset and wealth managment AUM by investor base
![](images/c43f2527d7bf1e66c4601f3a76208d4aed52531378ccb47cbcd0d27117920bbc.jpg)
Note: Excl. REITs and assets held under trusts attributable to non-LCs/RIs

In September 2021, the Cross-boundary Wealth Management Connect (WMC) scheme was launched, allowing eligible investors in the Greater Bay Area (GBA) to invest in wealth management products distributed by banks in each other<sup>’</sup>s market through a closed-loop funds flow channel. In January 2024, enhancement measures were announced to expand the scope of participating institutions and eligible products, and to increase the individual investor quota, etc. As of February 2024, accumulated amount of the remittance through the cross-border WMC reached RMB15.3bn, more than five times the level from a year ago.

On the other hand, challenges have emerged, as total AUM seems to have peaked in 2021 for the AWM business. Note that 2022 presented a challenging environment for global investors, with global AUM falling by \~10%. However, it seems the headwind hit the AWM business in HK more than its global peers, as the total AUM fell \~14% in 2022. In particular, AUM for the private banking (PB) and private wealth management (PWM) business peaked in 2020 at HK\$11.3 trillion in 2020, then fell to HK\$9.0 trillion in 2022. This is possibly related with the weak stock market performance and slowing IPO activities, as the pace of wealth creation slowed.

## Concerns over HK’s IFC status

The financial sector is probably the most important sector to Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s economy. From the year 2008 to 2021, the finance and insurance sectors contributed on average 0.8%pt to the 2.0%y/y real GDP growth each year. During the same period, the GDP share of the finance and insurance sectors has risen from 14% to more than 20% (Figure 5), making it the largest sector of HK<sup>’</sup>s economy. Meanwhile, total asset of the banking sector to nominal GDP ratio rose from 7.1x in end-2012 to 9.9x in end-2020. In 2024, Hong Kong is ranked 4<sup>th</sup> global financial center, following New York City, London, and Singapore, as indicated by the Global Financial Centers Index (GFCI 35).

Figure 5: HK GDP of financial and insurance sectors
![](images/849401f1bbef6f873b5196f4c1e92c47a32ae8a95c56078f546eda643e54faf1.jpg)

However, there have been rising concerns over Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s status as international financial center. Comparing with the 2018 ranking (GFCI 24), the deterioration of HK<sup>’</sup>s competitiveness as a financial center is prominent. HK was ranked top three in business environment, human capital, infrastructure, financial sector development, and reputation & general in 2018, and has fallen beyond top five in 2024. In addition to the poor performance in the stock market and the relatively muted IPO activities in 2023, total assets of Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s banking sector fell from 9.9x of GDP in end-2020 to 9.1x in end-2023. More critically, multinational companies seems to have begun trickling out of HK. According to HK C&SD, the city hosted 1,336 regional headquarters for multinational companies in 2023, vs. 1,541 regional headquarters in 2019 (link). Also, the number of total employees of regional headquarters has fallen from 195k in 2019 to 132k in 2023.

Meanwhile, HK<sup>’</sup>s financial sector has underperformed its overall economic growth. The real GDP of the financial &

insurance sectors fell by 2.6%y/y and 1.7%y/y in 2022 and
2023, respectively, and the share in total GDP also fell from
20.7% in 2020 to 19.6% in 2023. Note that HK<sup>’</sup>s real GDP in
2023 is still below its 2018 level, after the social unrest in
2019 and the pandemic.

The driving factors behind the challenging environment for Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s financial sector are both cyclical and structural. In our view, the cyclical factors include the high interest rate environment, while the structural factors include mainland China<sup>’</sup>s disinflation pressure and the slowdown of its potential growth, as well as the rising geopolitical tensions.

## Cyclical factor: high interest rate

The Federal Funds Rate was raised by 525bp in the current hike cycle since early 2022, more quickly and aggressively than in the previous one. Under the Linked Exchange Rate System (LERS), HK dollar is pegged to US dollar within the 7.75-7.85 range, and the HK dollar interest rates must follow their US counterparts. The high interest rate environment has depressed asset valuation and investment activities. Our US team expect the FOMC to start lower rates at the July meeting, but there is a clear risk that the start of the easing cycle could come later (link). This means that the high interest rate environment in HK will remain in the near team.

Given the trilemma of monetary policy, Hong Kong chooses fixed exchange rates and free flow of capital, and gives up the independent monetary policy. The USD-peg is a visible symbol of the “ one-country two-system<sup>”</sup> , and has enabled Hong Kong to become an international financial center. As long as the China-US business cycles were synchronized, this was not a major problem (1997-2007). However, it has become troublesome since the GFC and more so post-pandemic.

Figure 6: 1M SOFR vs. HIBOR
![](images/e0673bfb90da46ccd54435245979b8b127919aed8a28fc783cda802e1504beba.jpg)

Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s domestic demand remained weak in 2023, and the SOFR-HIBOR differential (Figure 6) has incentivized carry trades, which led to the selling of HK dollars and repeated triggering of the weak-side Convertibility Undertaking. The Aggregate Balance, a measure of interbank liquidity, has remained at a historical low level since May 2023, and Hong

Kong has lost 15% of its FX reserves as of March 2023 compared with the peak level in Nov 2021.

## Structural factors

In mainland China, structural imbalances have contributed to prolonged demand-supply imbalances and lingering deflation pressure. Recent CPI and PPI data pointed at over-supply concerns and lack of pricing power in the corporate sector. Despite the upside surprise in 1Q real GDP growth at 5.3%oya, nominal GDP growth came in on the soft side at 4.2%oya, with GDP deflator falling 1.1%oya. We expect the relative softness in China<sup>’</sup>s nominal GDP to continue in the coming quarters (Figure 7). As such, the pass-through from improved real activity to improved corporate earnings will still take time.

Figure 7: Nominal GDP and GDP deflator
![](images/2408fc8510ef01b78883a0df8fd76ecb644fb085460c4fe7151e0fc736b6d4c7.jpg)

A longer-term challenge is mainland China<sup>’</sup>s slowing potential growth. We have recently revised our estimates, which show a faster deceleration of potential growth to below 4% in 2025 , and also a lower potential growth that averages 3.6% in 2026-30. Both the deflationary pressure and the slowing potential growth dragged Chinese companies<sup>’</sup> profitability and valuation, as well as the performance of Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s stock market. As mentioned above, Chinese companies account for more than 70% of the total market capitalization of Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s stock market.

Geopolitical tension is weighing on the growth of Hong Kong. According to the IMF<sup>’</sup>s research, geopolitical relationships seem to matter for allocating cross-border capital, with investors generally allocating a smaller share of capital to recipient countries with more distant foreign policy outlooks to their country of origin. This may partly explain the larger deterioration in the AUM of Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s AWM business compared with the global peers. Also, international companies have been moving their regional headquarters and business out of Hong Kong, so as to mitigate the potential loss and collateral damage from the geopolitical risks.

The introduction of the National Security Law on HK SAR and passing of Article 23 have raised market concerns on the city<sup>’</sup>s autonomous legal framework. CE Lee said at the Legislative Council that passing the bill would allow HK to focus on developing the economy. The government has also been trying to ensure the market that the bill only targets an extremely small minority of people who endanger national security, thus normal business operators won<sup>’</sup>t be affected (link). However, it may take some time to convince the market that the bill will not affect Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s common law framework.

## Outlooks

While many of the driving factors are exogenous to the city<sup>’</sup>s domestic policies, their dynamics of impacts are not all developing into a worse condition for HK. On interest rates, there are uncertainties regarding the timing and pace of the Fed rate cut outlook, but the high interest rate environment is unlikely to ever-present. On mainland China<sup>’</sup>s prolonged demand-supply imbalances, lingering deflation pressure and slowing potential growth, the market seems to have already priced in these negative factors, and the Chinese companies<sup>’</sup> valuation has been at discounts (see our equity strategy team<sup>’</sup>s recent research here). The downsides seem to be limited. On growing geopolitical tensions, one positive side effect is, Hong Kong may become a safer and more popular IPO destination for the Chinese companies. Meanwhile, a possible signal of turnaround is, the number of U.S. citizens living in HK in 2023 has rebounded to approximately the pre-pandemic level (see US Dept. of State<sup>’</sup>s 2024 HK Policy Act Report).

Looking back, the status of a separate customs territory, business-friendly taxation, and the USD-peg have made HK an international financial and trade center. Through the years, the city has been enhancing its financial infrastructure, while recent talents attraction schemes have been launched to maintain the human capital. Moreover, though the pace of growth slowed, the size of mainland China<sup>’</sup>s economy and the need for financial service are still enormous, with plenty of opportunities for HK SAR<sup>’</sup>s financial sector.

As HK<sup>’</sup>s financial secretary Paul Chan put it recently (link), given the current situation, the unique status of HK under the “one-country-two-systems<sup>”</sup> structure has become even more important. The legal system, the free flow of capital, the USD-peg, the low and simple tax system, and the status of a separate customs territory provide advantages to HK over other Chinese cities. HK has also been seeking new opportunities in Green Finance, financial innovation, and attracting global family offices and asset owners. We think HK will remain the leading RMB offshore business hub, and it is in HK<sup>’</sup>s interest to integrate into the Greater Bay Area, and further deepen its financial linkages with the Mainland.

## United States

• Fed officials confirm view that rate hikes are not the baseline

• No change in our main views with limited data news

• Next week, we look for April core CPI to be up 0.3%

• We forecast decent retail sales headline with control group up 0.2%

The economic news was quiet this week following a lot of events during the prior week and ahead of a busy few days of data reports scheduled for next week. With limited new information, our main view on the economy did not really change. We did see comments from a variety of Fed officials, who had the opportunity to update their views following the April employment report from May 3. Unsurprisingly, there doesn<sup>’</sup>t appear to be a major reaction coming from the relatively dovish news in that report. The messages from recent Fedspeak were largely consistent with earlier communication, as Fed officials generally seem to view rates as being restrictive and the biggest issue on the immediate agenda is deciding how long to keep rates on hold at their current levels before eventually starting to lower them. While easing remains the base case for the next move, a few speakers kept open the possibility of rate hikes at some point in the future.

At the latest FOMC meeting, Chair Powell indicated an unexpected weakening in the labor market could lead the Fed to start cutting rates. But despite modest disappointment in last week<sup>’</sup>s May employment report and a bump up in initial jobless claims this week, we don<sup>’</sup>t think the FOMC will view these data as concerning unless further weakening follows. Although initial claims filings rose to their highest weekly level since late August, we think this increase will at least partially prove to be temporary, as it reflects a jump in filings associated with recent spring breaks (that was not anticipated by the seasonal factors for the data). Moreover, claims can be volatile on a week-to-week basis; more noteworthy would be a sustained break above the current benign trend.

Another potential path towards rate cuts, as indicated by Powell, would be increased confidence that inflation is coming down sustainably. In this respect, next week<sup>’</sup>s April CPI report will be important, particularly as it is the only one before the June FOMC meeting that Fed officials will be able to publicly comment on (the May CPI report will be released on the second day of the June meeting). We forecast that the headline CPI increased 0.4% and that the core index increased 0.3% (0.33% to two decimals). While not a particularly soft change in the core index, we think that the April change will be the lowest month-on-month print since December and it will allow the related year-ago reading to

continue to moderate (hitting 3.7% in April). Officials are also likely monitoring a variety of inflation expectation measures, and the preliminary May University of Michigan report showed increased consumer inflation expectations this month.

While not as important as the CPI report, the April retail sales data also will be useful to track next week. We believe the headline increased 0.6% and we expect more modest growth in the important control group, with this aggregate up 0.2%. Other releases next week include a variety of May business surveys and the April IP report. Powell is also scheduled to speak on May 14 and other Fed officials will make remarks at various points during the upcoming week.

## Claims jump is probably noise

Initial claims jumped from 209,000 to 231,000 during the week ending May 4, a disappointing print that showed the highest weekly level for claims filings since last August. We had been expecting an increase in filings in this report (although not as large as the one that was actually reported) because we thought that the seasonal adjustment of the data would not fully capture temporary changes in filings that were associated with school spring breaks, and we continue to think that this latest increase in filings at least partially reflects noise in the data. An increase in filings in New York State was a big driver of the national increase (Figure 1), and we think that the move up in New York was at least partially related to filings associated with recent school spring breaks for New York City public schools. The seasonal adjustment process has a hard time correctly anticipating the swings in filings that come around school spring breaks (and “moving holidays<sup>”</sup> like Easter and Passover), so the seasonally adjusted data often are noisy around this time of year.

![](images/15fcbb4462001b0ebf2305e535c446d3ea9f673ac10ca88edc0177f5e5013af6.jpg)

Stepping back a bit, we should also keep in mind that the latest level of initial claims filings is still fairly low by broad historical standards, even though it was the highest seen in several months. Continuing claims filings also generally have been low lately, and the latest report showed related filings increasing from 1.768mn to 1.785mn during the week ending April 27. Low levels of jobless claims filings suggest continued strength in the labor market, although several other measures point to some relative cooling lately.

## Inflation expectations move up in May

The preliminary May University of Michigan consumer survey showed increases in inflation expectations early in the month. The median one-year-ahead measure rose from 3.2% in April to 3.5%, coming in at its highest level since November. While there has been clear firming in this measure very recently, it remains 0.7%-pt below its reading last May and it is lower than almost all of the figures between mid-2021 and mid-2023. The survey<sup>’</sup>s longer-term measure has also moved up lately, with the median five-year-ahead inflation expectation rising 0.1%pt to 3.1% in May, on top of the 0.2%-pt increase reported for April. The latest print for this measure is now just a tenth below the high from recent years (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Michigan survey inflation expectations
![](images/391dc9716f76581f10e3ca4deb972362423f39831e40c0a880f17be0dea815f3.jpg)

The Michigan survey also showed a disappointing print on consumer sentiment, with a sizable decline in the headline index for May (-9.8-pts to 67.4). While sentiment has turned lower in recent months (Figure 3), consumer spending still appears to have had solid momentum lately, though our views on the consumer will be influenced by how the April retail sales report performs next week.

Figure 3: University of Michigan consumer sentiment index
![](images/ccd8e0d323324ece0b66f5ffa81d9222b1fc6666f2064a4f2c33aa0296658537.jpg)

## SLOOS is back from the brink

The changes in the latest quarterly Senior Loan Officer Opinion Survey (SLOOS) were somewhat mixed. More broadly, in recent quarters the main series have moved away from a period with intense tightening in lending standards and intense declines in demand (Figure 4). While there are many factors that influence our views on recession risks, the move towards less extreme data in the SLOOS on its own makes it seem less likely that a recession would start in the relatively near term. That said, the continued tightening in lending standards and the continued weakening in demand reported in the survey are consistent with the economy losing some steam over time.

Figure 4: SLOOS responses on C&I loans to large/medium firms Net % tightening standards or reporting stronger demand
![](images/93e4982c328a05c82dfc29f4963868942551100594ecc0057adb19ce5fcc47f6.jpg)

## Inventories a little softer

We didn<sup>’</sup>t receive much news on the GDP front, but there were downward revisions reported for recent data on wholesale inventories (Figure 5). As a result, we lowered our tracking estimate of 1Q real GDP growth from 1.6% to 1.4% saar. We continue to look for a step up in growth in 2Q (to 2.5% saar), but we think that the broader trend in GDP growth should be softening through some of the quarterly noise. Of course, we have limited hard data regarding 2Q GDP at this point.

Figure 5: Wholesale inventories
![](images/16ddc6df3ec480c21ac1bb9b41478abe848455276bdc317187cba077041c4700.jpg)

We look for the producer price index (PPI) to have risen 0.3% in April, which would coincide with a year-ago increase of 2.2%. Recent firming in food and energy prices has contributed to an upward trajectory to PPI since the start of the year, and we look for continued firmness in food prices last month (0.7%) as well as a strong rise in energy prices (1.4%), largely on the back of a rise in gasoline prices.

Away from food and energy, we anticipate a 0.2% rise in core producer prices in April, with the corresponding year-ago inflation rate edging down to 2.3%oya—the first move lower in this measure since the start of this year. Moderation in core services PPI prices, which we see having risen 0.2% in April, accounts for most of the modest easing in the pace of core PPI inflation. We also look for a 0.2% increase in core goods prices, which would keep the monthly (and annual) pace of growth fairly range-bound.
