1. Total ex. gasoline, automotive dealers, building materials, and food serv.

We estimate that nominal retail sales rose a solid 0.6% in April, in part on the back of price increases that should have boosted nominal sales at gasoline stations (estimated to have risen 1.9% last month). Autos-related sales at vehicle dealers and at parts stores also were likely strong in April (up 1.3%) as the impacts of supply-chain disruptions fade further. Sales of the closely-watched control group, which excludes autos, building materials, gasoline, and food, likely gained a more modest 0.2% last month based on our Chase spending card data. That increase would follow an outsized 1.1% monthly jump in March; some cooling was likely after such a strong reading and would be consistent with softer April employment and business survey data.

<table><tr><td>Wed CPI May 15 8:30am Total</td><td>%m/m, sa, unless noted</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Jan</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Feb 0.4</td><td>Mar 0.4</td><td>Apr 0.4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>0.3 3.1</td><td>3.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%oya (nsa) Core</td><td></td><td>3.5 0.36</td><td>3.4</td></tr><tr><td>%oya (nsa)</td><td>0.39 3.9</td><td>0.36 3.8</td><td>0.33 3.7</td></tr><tr><td>Core services</td><td>0.7</td><td>3.8 0.5 0.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Core goods</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Food</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.1 0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Energy</td><td>-0.9</td><td>2.3 1.1</td><td>1.6</td></tr><tr><td>Housing</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.4 0.4</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Owners&#x27; eq.rent</td><td>0.56</td><td>0.44 0.44</td><td>0.41</td></tr><tr><td>Rent</td><td>0.36</td><td>0.46 0.41</td><td>0.39</td></tr><tr><td>Lodging away from home</td><td>1.8</td><td>0.1 0.1</td><td>-0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Apparel</td><td>-0.7</td><td>0.6 0.7</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>New vehicles</td><td>0.0</td><td>-0.1 -0.2</td><td>-0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Used vehicles</td><td>-3.4</td><td>0.5 -1.1</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Public transportation</td><td>1.3</td><td>2.3 -1.0</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Communication</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.5</td><td>-0.1 0.0</td></tr><tr><td>Medical care</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.5 0.4</td></tr></table>

We forecast that the consumer price index (CPI) rose 0.4% in April, with the year-ago inflation rate easing to 3.4% from 3.5%oya in March. A 1.6% expected monthly rise in energy prices, led by a further climb in gasoline prices, should have contributed meaningfully to the headline CPI gain in April— even as electricity and natural gas service prices fell on the month. Meanwhile food inflation was likely soft last month, rising just 0.1% for the second straight month. Excluding food and energy, we look for core CPI to have risen 0.3% in April (0.33% to two decimal places). This pace would be the lowest of the year, but still above every monthly reading from June through December 2023. If this forecast is realized, the year-ago pace would continue its very gradual deceleration to reach 3.7%oya in April from 3.8% in February and March.

While we look for slightly softer rental inflation measures in April, the monthly readings are still expected to remain firm. We anticipate a 0.41% monthly gain in owner<sup>’</sup>s equivalent rent while tenants<sup>’</sup> rent likely increased 0.39%. Although industry measures suggest new lease rates continue to come in soft to weak, these represent only a portion of total rental rates captured in the CPI measures, and rent extensions appear to be growing at a faster pace. Elsewhere among the shelter measures, industry data point to a modest easing of lodging prices (-0.4%)—the first decline in five months in the seasonally adjusted measure.

Recent months have also seen some sizable declines in vehicle prices, which we expect continued in April for new vehicles (-0.2%) whereas industry data point to used vehicle prices having effectively stabilized (up 0.1% in seasonally adjusted terms) after another large drop in March. In other transportation-related components, we look for a small rise in public transportation prices after a large fall in March that only partially reversed several months of notably strong prints.

Some idiosyncratic factors should account for expected shifts in other CPI price series last month. We expect growth of medical inflation prices to begin to slow in April and continue for the next six months. But firmness in other medical care costs should keep the growth of this component elevated at 0.4% in April. Communication prices should be effectively unchanged in April after increases in postage stamp prices in the first two months of the year pushed this measure up significantly. But another round of increases were already announced in April, to take effect in July. Among the other CPI components, we expect apparel prices eased, down 0.1% in April after a few months of unexpectedly strong prints.

We forecast that initial jobless claims declined 6,000 to 225,000 during the week ending May 11. <table><tr><td rowspan="5">Thu May 16 8:30am Starts</td><td>Housing starts Million units, saar</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jan 1.38</td><td>Feb 1.55</td><td>Mar 1.32</td><td>Apr 1.35</td></tr><tr><td>1.02</td><td>1.17</td><td>1.02</td><td>1.01</td></tr><tr><td>0.36</td><td>0.38</td><td>0.30</td><td>0.34</td></tr><tr><td>Multifamily starts Permits</td><td>1.49</td><td>1.52</td><td>1.47</td><td>1.45</td></tr></table>

We estimate housing starts rose 2.2% to 1.350mn saar in April while permits slipped 1.2% to 1.450mn saar. After a big decline in 2022 as the Fed start to tighten policy, single-family starts and permits had been trending higher since early last year but have turned more volatile lately; the recent move back up in mortgage rates may again be dragging on housing activity. Single-family starts dropped sharply in March after a large gain in February; some of that volatility may have been weather-related and we look for a modest further move down in April of 1.2%. We also expect a small further decline of 0.8% for single-family permits in April. Meanwhile, the multifamily data are typically more volatile and we look for a sizable 13.8% jump in multifamily starts in April after a large decline in March; multifamily permits are expected to have slowed modestly further (-1.9%) last month as well.

<table><tr><td rowspan="5">Thu May 16 8:30am</td><td rowspan="2">Import prices %m/m, nsa, unless noted</td><td colspan="4"></td></tr><tr><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td></tr><tr><td>Import prices</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>-1.3</td><td>-0.9</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>0.6</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Ex-fuel import prices %oya</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.0 0.1</td></tr></table>

We think the import price index rose 0.3% (non-seasonally adjusted) in April, and 0.4% over the past year. Higher imported energy prices on the month account for much of this gain; we look a flat nonfuel import price index in April and a 0.1% year-ago increase. Import prices on a year-ago basis had been declining at a decent clip throughout all of 2023 and into early this year, but that disinflationary impulse appears to have largely faded now despite recent US dollar strength.

<table><tr><td>Thu May 16</td><td>Philadelphia Fed survey Diffusion indices, sa</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="7">8:30am</td><td></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>General bus. conditions</td><td>5.2</td><td>3.2</td><td>15.5</td><td>8.0</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>-5.2</td><td>5.4</td><td>12.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Shipments</td><td>10.7 -2.8</td><td>11.4 4.4</td><td>19.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Inventories Prices paid</td><td>16.6</td><td>3.7</td><td>-8.9 23.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Prices received</td><td>6.2</td><td>4.6</td><td>5.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Composite</td><td>47.1</td><td>49.5</td><td>50.2</td><td></td></tr></table>

We forecast that the Philadelphia Fed manufacturing survey<sup>’</sup>s headline declined from 15.5 in April to 8.0 in May. The firming reported for April brought the headline measure up to its highest level in about two years. While things may be picking up in the manufacturing sector, the message from the broader set of business surveys has been mixed, with several looking downbeat lately. We therefore think we will see some moderation in the May data from the Philadelphia Fed survey following the solid improvement reported for April.

<table><tr><td>Thu May 16 9:15am</td><td>Industrial production</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m,sa, unless noted</td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar Apr</td></tr><tr><td>Industrial production</td><td>-0.8</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.4 0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Manufacturing</td><td>-1.1</td><td>1.2 0.5</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Motor vehicles and parts</td><td>-3.8</td><td>3.4</td><td>3.1 -0.7</td></tr><tr><td>High-tech</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-1.1</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Mfg ex motor vehicles</td><td>-0.9</td><td>1.0</td><td>0.3 0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Business equipment</td><td>-1.0</td><td>2.0</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Capacity utilization (%,sa)</td><td>77.9</td><td>78.2</td><td>78.4 78.4</td></tr><tr><td>Manufacturing</td><td>76.3</td><td>77.1 77.4</td><td>77.4</td></tr></table>

We look for industrial production to have edged up 0.1% in April, with manufacturing output likely having expanded 0.2% last month. If realized, these readings would suggest somewhat less industrial sector momentum relative to readings in the latter months of 1Q. After a few strong months of auto sector production, industry data suggest a 0.7% pullback in April; that would leave manufacturing ex. motor vehicles and parts up 0.2% last month. Meanwhile, we expect mining to contract again in April, down 1.2%, as hours worked contracted in the sector last month, while utilities should be up 1.3%. Both moves are similar to the month prior.

## Consumer sentiment (May 10)

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Michigan preliminary</td><td rowspan="2">Apr</td><td rowspan="2">May</td><td rowspan="2"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mar 79.4</td></tr><tr><td>Univ. of Mich. Index (nsa)</td><td></td><td>77.2</td><td>77.5</td><td>67.4</td></tr><tr><td>Current conditions</td><td>82.5</td><td>79.0</td><td></td><td>68.8</td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>77.4</td><td>76.0</td><td></td><td>66.5</td></tr><tr><td>Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Short-term</td><td>2.9</td><td>3.2</td><td></td><td>3.5</td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td>2.8</td><td>3.0</td><td></td><td>3.1</td></tr></table>

The University of Michigan consumer survey showed a disappointing print on sentiment in the preliminary May report along with increases in the main inflation expectation measures. The headline sentiment index fell from 77.2 in April to 67.4, which was a pretty sizable drop that brought the index down to its lowest level since November. The latest reading is still up 8.4pts from where it was last May, but that earlier print was one of the weakest on record for the survey. In terms of the inflation expectation measures, the median oneyear-ahead expectation increased from 3.2% in April to 3.5% in May while the median five-year-ahead measure rose from 3.0% to 3.1%. The shorter-term measure is now at its highest level since November, although it remains down 0.7%pt over the past year and lower than many of the figures reported between mid-2021 and mid-2023. The longer-term inflation expectation measure has now moved up 0.3%pt over the past two months alone (according to the preliminary May figure which could be revised), with the latest figure down just a tenth off of the high from recent years.

## Focus: Construction demand for labor cooling off

The construction sector accounts for about 5% of total nonfarm payrolls but it generated about 8% of the payroll gains last year, around 20k per month (Figure 1). So far in 2024, almost 10% of overall payroll growth has been in construction (25K per month average), significantly above its share of overall employment. That is probably why many observers were concerned by the sharp decline in job openings in the construction sector in March. Of course, one month<sup>’</sup>s change like this can be partly noise. However, we think that this unusually large drop might be at least partially related to softening demand for labor in this sector that can be traced to anticipated large numbers of completions of housing units under construction and a leveling off of the boom we experienced in nonresidential structures recently. This normalization of underlying labor demand seems consistent with monthly payroll gains of around 5,000 in 2Q and flat readings in the second half.

Figure 1: Payroll growth 000s, 3mma sa - both scales
![](images/816a1f9e68ecb097ec91ad99d517556922afa3405449a72760e573e5c1d4ebb6.jpg)

The March drop in construction job openings (182K) was the largest in the sample going back to 2001 when the JOLTS data started. The level of job openings is essentially back to a recent low we saw in February 2021. Note that prior to the decline, over the past year job openings in the construction sector actually fluctuated a lot but stayed relatively firm. Also, compared to pre-pandemic, job openings in the construction sector did not increase as much as overall vacancies did during the early recovery either. Thus labor demand did not cool off as discernibly in the construction industry between mid-2022 and the last print. JOLTS data also suggest that layoffs in this sector remain relatively low (not shown) indicating a likely adjustment through the recruitment margin and natural turnover, rather than an imminent jump in displacements, at least so far.

Figure 2: Job openings sa level normalized to February 2020=100
![](images/856bf3849c5e1ea108c2556a808b966baeed08b9f18c503e33fb627ac54a4ce0.jpg)

We think that cooling demand will come as more housing units under construction are completed (Figure 3). As we have recently argued, a large number of the housing units under construction are due to be completed fairly soon, and we could see completions over the next couple of months average a solid annualized rate of about 1.5mn per month, which would be consistent with lower labor demand. Aside from this softening labor demand on the residential side, we also think demand will be somewhat softer due to leveling off in nonresidential structures, which likely helped boost payroll growth in the construction sector over the past year (Figure 4). The incentives provided by legislation enacted in 2022, such as the CHIPS Act or IRA, provided some support for structures, which has leveled off recently. These two channels together produce the slowdown in construction employment we project for the rest of the year.

Figure 3: Construction employment and housing under construction q/q change, 000s sa Monthly avg. 00
![](images/b40ceb28f346d2d5381f6617704204c5fb5433991cf85b792996850541ecd951.jpg)

Figure 4: Construction employment and nonresidential structures %ch, saar Monthly avg. 000s sa
![](images/47da66733369b63add5ed21ea627a38441bd8ebc4893f779dfe02b5e6d58b225.jpg)

## Euro Area

• ECB minutes consistent with a June cut

• Retail sales up, but increase linked to volatile elements

• Industrial production likely up 0.6%m/m sa next week

• Wage inflation likely moderated in 1Q

The account of the ECB<sup>’</sup>s April meeting and this week<sup>’</sup>s data look consistent with the ECB cutting rates in June. A modest upward revision in the April PMI reinforces the sense of improving growth momentum. Coming alongside a pickup in the PMI employment index, the implication is that labor productivity growth remains weak and unit labor cost growth biased upwards. Combined with the likelihood that Euro area core inflation could print 0.2%-pt higher than the ECB staff<sup>’</sup>s forecast in 2Q24, the data may at first sight be challenging the ECB<sup>’</sup>s narrative of disinflationary progress.

But, the account of the ECB<sup>’</sup>s April meeting suggests that the ECB is focused on the broad direction of travel and this is likely still in the direction of further disinflation. Last week, we already noted that national data are pointing to slower growth in the GDP deflator. Whilst this is from a high level, it does hint at more modest gains in unit profits, which the ECB is likely to be encouraged by. In particular, the April meeting account still mentioned this part of the staff<sup>’</sup>s forecast as a key uncertainty.

Similarly, national data on negotiated pay in 1Q24 have lowered our tracking estimate a tick to 4.3%oya, which is below the peak of 4.7%oya in 3Q23. And this slowing is coming despite an acceleration in Germany, where delayed pay settlements are still driving a pickup. But, as the German pickup partly reflects inflation compensation payments in recently negotiated deals, it is consistent with our call that most of the pickup in Euro area wage growth reflects a response to high past inflation, rather than to tightness in the labor market per se. If correct, then the fact that headline inflation has already slowed sharply should allow wage growth to moderate further in the coming quarters. While this remains an uncertain process, it seems that the ECB is sufficiently encouraged to at least start reducing rates.

## Reasons to fade retail sales increase

Euro area retail sales increased 0.8%m/m sa in March, a tick above our forecast, which left the 1Q level of retail sales 0.3% ar above the 4Q level. This second quarterly increase in a row comes after seven consecutive quarters of contraction. While retail sales were on a steep trend down between 1Q22 and 3Q23, the trend was largely flat in recent months (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Euro area consumer spending
![](images/b45886fde465558d1573772d54df8038f9cbcf5440a3b7c9b95a7aad33af7db4.jpg)

The March increase broke the recent trend, but we prefer to fade this increase to some extent. Looking at the details, the March increase was skewed towards food (up 1.2%m/m) and fuel (up 2.0%m/m) while the rest of the basket was flat. At the country level (Figure 2), the March increase also was skewed towards Germany (up 1.8%m/m after a 1.6% decline the prior month), a country where retail sales data can be very volatile. In the other main economies of the region, retail sales were up 1.1%m/m in France (the only main economy where retail sales have been trending upward of late) and 0.1%m/m in Italy. Meanwhile, retail sales were down 0.6% in Spain. Limited details available with preliminary country-level 1Q GDP estimates last week hinted at decent household gains likely driven by the services sector. In our view, strong income gains should continue to support household spending in the coming months.

Figure 2: Euro area retail sales
![](images/c9771119ebd9504ab1a2f23afdda5d79326fb068673ee02e7eb7e07cc70943c5.jpg)

## March IP likely up next week

The Euro area March IP release is due on Wednesday, but a large number of countries already published data that we can use to assess the Euro area print. Among the main economies of the region (Figure 3), IP ex. construction declined in Germany (-0.7%m/m), France (-0.3%m/m) Italy (-0.5%m/m) and Spain (-1.0%m/m). Meanwhile, manufacturing output jumped 15.7%m/m in Ireland, where IP can be volatile, due to the owning of production facilities outside the country and patent transfers.

Figure 3: Euro area IP ex. construction
![](images/6be57c1e70b3692ce69e9dd3bae33327be22150e9c5b0cdd22c6dba00c7972a0.jpg)

Using country-level data, we estimate that Euro area IP ex. construction was likely up 0.6%m/m sa in March, which would leave the 1Q24 level 4.4% ar below the 4Q23 level due to a large 3.0%m/m IP fall in January. Regarding manufacturing, we estimate a 2.3%m/m sa jump, which would leave the 1Q24 level 6.2% ar below the 4Q23 level. These estimates are impacted to a large degree by Ireland. Excluding Ireland, Euro area IP ex. construction was, we think, down 0.4%m/m in March with manufacturing down 0.3%m/m, leaving 1Q broadly flat compared to 4Q (-0.3% ar and 0.2% ar respectively).

Excluding Ireland, Euro area IP ex. construction was on a steep trend down last year (Figure 4). The most recent data show some signs of stabilization with a modest recovery in energy-intensive industries in some countries (as gas prices still remain low). The PMI has not been a good indicator for manufacturing output in recent quarters, but the data through the start of 2Q continue to hint at a weak manufacturing sector in the region.

Figure 4: Euro area industrial production ex. construction Dec 2019 = 100
![](images/d8e7b836399fe1eec6c2699d3804e1f881f9c7f593710681893c688822a48660.jpg)

## Wages show signs of moderation

The ECB<sup>’</sup>s quarterly indicator of negotiated pay will not be available for another two weeks, but most of the national data feeding into it are already available for 1Q24. Our aggregation currently points to an outcome of 4.3%oya, which would be below the 4.5%oya in 4Q23 and the peak of 4.7%oya in 3Q23 (Figure 5). The main missing data point is the Bundes-

bank<sup>’</sup>s index for March.

Figure 5: Euro area wage growth
![](images/2a05c05c09ae89d5db1cb2eb8d68b61888d36a4740a00f99f5fd1e43cf9f9252.jpg)

While the moderation is modest so far, it reflects an important rotation. Negotiated pay growth is moving lower in quite a few countries, while lagged pay settlements and pay rises in Germany are still pushing its index higher. However, the German index is effectively still being lifted by inflation compensation payments in old deals that are being negotiated to include a response to the past inflation surge. Over time, this effect should also fade. Of course, the exact landing zone of Euro area wage growth is still hard to gauge, but we maintain our assumption that a large part of the pick-up in wage growth reflects a response to the inflation surge rather than to the tight labour market per se. This underpins our view that Euro area inflation has a good chance of eventually moderating back to 2%, even in an environment of trend-like GDP growth.

The incoming wage data are likely broadly in line with the ECB staff projections, which show a relatively firm quarterly gain in effective pay in 1Q24, followed by steady moderation in momentum during the rest of this year. Instead, the pressure point is that productivity growth is showing little sign of picking up so far alongside the improvement in GDP growth, which biases unit labour cost growth to the upside, but for now, the ECB is likely to take some encouragement from the wage data.

## Japan

The yen continued to slide this week, posing renewed challenges to the BoJ

• The weak yen likely will expand the growth imbalance, posing downside risks to the recovery in 2Q24

We expect the BoJ to announce a reduction of JGB purchases in June, followed by a rate hike in July

• Next week<sup>’</sup>s 1Q24 GDP to highlight growth imbalance, with resident capex and weak consumption

Despite strong tailwinds from the weaker yen, Japan<sup>’</sup>s economic recovery remains biased to the corporate sector. Despite growing momentum for wage hikes, wage growth has yet to catch up with inflation as firms remain reluctant to raise labor costs under the rigid employment practices, which is prolonging the drag on households<sup>’</sup> real income. We expect next week<sup>’</sup>s 1Q24 GDP to highlight this imbalance, showing a contrast between a slump in private consumption and imports and the resilience in capex. The ongoing yen weakening likely will expand this imbalance further, posing downside risks to the expected recovery toward the middle of the year. This week<sup>’</sup>s April print for SME sentiment highlighted the risk, falling to a near two-year low in April, against the backdrop of rising costs and sluggish consumption due to the weaker yen.

In order to help close this imbalance, the BoJ has committed to maintain an accommodative monetary policy even after an achievement of the stable price target was in sight. However, the persistent pressures on the yen present a renewed challenge to this approach, through posing upside risks to inflation which will further undermine households<sup>’</sup> purchasing power. The April BoJ opinions support our view of a policy rate of 50bp by year-end. They reiterated the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s base case strategy of a gradual policy normalization, bringing the policy rate back to neutral within the second half of their forecast horizon of FY2024-2026. But there are several opinions hinting that risks are tilted towards an earlier move.

With the strong global economic backdrop leading to a weak yen trend, as well as the stronger-than-expected result of the spring wage negotiation, many members saw upside risks to the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s inflation forecasts. Those risks have risen even higher, with the yen continuing to slide this week even after MoF intervention. We believe the BoJ would rather tolerate weakening of the yen, as long as the pace remains modest, as a rapid rate hike would become a significant drag on the economy as a whole. However, with the rising risks of capital outflows and inflation, the BoJ likely will be urged to move modestly faster.On policy sequencing, we expect the BoJ to deliver a decision on a reduction of JGB purchase in June, as suggested by the Opinions. With regard to rate hikes, our confidence in our forecast of the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s next move to 25bp in July has risen, with rising upside risks to the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s inflation outlook.

## BoJ sees risks of faster moves

The Summary of Opinions at the April Monetary Policy Meeting showed general consensus among the members on the further policy normalization. As for additional rate hikes, an opinion stated that “there is a possibility that the future policy interest rate will be higher than the path that is factored in by the market.<sup>”</sup> We think this came from one of the Governors or Deputy Governors, as this is in line with the remarks stated by Governor Ueda at the post-meeting press conference. He noted “the prospect of the policy rate reaching around neutral sometime in the second half of the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s forecasts period.<sup>”</sup> Regarding the pace of rate hikes, the gradual approach with data dependency appears to have been broadly agreed upon, with some wordings such as “moderate policy interest rate hikes<sup>”</sup> and “accommodative financial conditions will be maintained for the time being.<sup>”</sup>

That said, there is an opinion clearly stating risks for a faster move: “if underlying inflation continues to deviate upward from the baseline scenario against the backdrop of a weaker yen, it is quite possible that the pace of monetary policy normalization will increase.<sup>”</sup> Others have remained cautious, pointing out the need to assess economic conditions, such as an improving trend in private consumption, before considering further adjustments. The BoJ Board members are apparently considering the timing for additional rate hikes, but still seem to prefer to take a gradual approach based on data.

With regard to asset purchases, all opinions listed supported a reduction. As for the JGB purchases, there is a strong indication that a Board decision to reduce its purchases should come sometime soon. Those include that “at some point it should indicate its intention to reduce its purchase amount of JGBs,<sup>”</sup> and that “it is important for the Bank to proceed with reducing its purchase amount of JGBs in a timely manner.<sup>”</sup> We have expected that a reduction in the JGB purchases will be decided soon, and we think these opinions support our view.

One surprise to us was that there were two opinions requesting a start of discussions on the treatment of the Bank's ETF holdings, although they stated “there is no simple solution to this issue,<sup>”</sup> and “it takes a long time.<sup>”</sup> We still think the BoJ is unlikely to make a decision on this anytime soon. But, at least two members seem to recognize that they have reached the stage of considering medium- to long-term exit strategies in a way that reduces market impact.

In terms of the price outlook, inflation upside risks were pointed out. Many were concerned about a rise in import prices. In addition, some also saw inflation upside risks arising from rising inflation expectations and a higher inflation-wage spiral. With the results of the spring wage negotiations coming out strongly, the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s risk bias to its inflation forecasts appears to be tilting upward.

## Anemic real wages drag consumption

Wage growth disappointed in March, with average wage growth rising a modest 0.6% oya. The February print was also revised down from 1.8% to 1.4%, leaving the average for 1Q24 at 1.2%. The March weakness was partly attributable to the base effect in special payments, which had jumped in the same month of 2023 with one-off payments to cover higher inflation at that time. Overtime payments also remained lackluster, falling 1.5%oya, but this is partly attributable to weak production activity. However, as was the case in the prior months, the underlying details look strong. Scheduled earnings rose 1.7%oya, led by a 5.0%oya rise in part-timers<sup>’</sup> pay (Figure 1). Constant sample data (which remove sampling volatility and are a focus for the BoJ) showed stronger growth, with a 2.2%oya rise. Stripping out choppy bonus and other special components, base pay levels continue to move up relatively sharply.

Figure 1: Scheduled earnings composition %oya, both scales
![](images/fc8d20fc0421b3d28d0bd472e6c836a3d026f4a3582408b57a3cd4b4ec98f6aa.jpg)
Source: MHLW, J.P. Morgan

We expect wage growth to continue to trend higher. The fifth round of the Shunto negotiations saw average base pay hikes settle at 3.57%, with even SMEs reporting over a 3% rise. We note this will not lead to an immediate jump in wage growth, as the results tend to be gradually translated into economywide scheduled pay growth. However, assuming that this year<sup>’</sup>s base pay hikes settle at the mid-3% mark, we think economy-wide full-time earnings growth is likely to trend up to 2.5%-3%oya towards the end of this year.

Despite the rising trend in wages, real labor income drags persisted even in March (Figure 2). With inflation running well above the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s target of 2%, real wage growth marked the 24<sup>th</sup> consecutive monthly drop, falling 2.5%oya in March.

This has translated into weak domestic consumption, and the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s Consumption Activity Index (CAI, travel balance adjusted) fell 0.3%m/m, sa, in March, leaving the 1Q24 average 2.0%q/q, saar below 4Q23. While consumption declines were broad-based in March, we see particular weakness in goods consumption, while services consumption remained relatively firm (Figure 3). Overall, this result indicated weak domestic consumption activity continuing to 1Q, due to persistent real income drags.

Figure 2: Total and scheduled earnings, real
![](images/8ecb59b53ba88c4b4bfef80dee5249db0ea5525a170329cdb2b090b70276bda3.jpg)

Figure 3: BoJ Consumption activity index 2015=100, sa
![](images/30fda54fb3b2f7e24135091f4c59d10a023b944cd3ea1b5f32332a8f17b72f2d.jpg)

Fugure 4: Real consumption %q/q, saar; dot denotes JPM forecasts
![](images/408e7c9e3cf0296770cc2203078c167133ffeafb457ec41db964e796afc96fda.jpg)

While we think CAI are not necessarily fully tracking GDPbased consumption, we see some downside risks to our 1Q GDP-based consumption forecast of -0.5%q/q, saar (Figure 4). While we continue to expect a recovery in real consumption starting from 2Q reflecting the recent strong wage momentum and a trend recovery in consumer sentiment, we see rising risks for this outlook. With the trend weakening of the yen, we now see some upside risks to inflation, potentially leading to real wage growth remaining negative for longer despite higher nominal wage growth.

The first estimate of 1Q24 GDP is likely to show a consecutive quarterly rise, with a 1%q/q, saar rise. Despite the plunge in production caused by factory shutdowns by an auto maker, capex activity appears to have remained solid with an expectation of future recovery. We expect consumption to have remained weak, marking the forth consecutive quarterly decline, reflecting the persistent real income drags. The expected declines in production and consumption, combined with the weaker yen, likely have pushed down imports, with a larger decline than in exports, resulting in a positive contribution of net exports to overall growth.

## Canada

• Employment increased by 90,400 in April, beating expectations

• Unemployment held steady at 6.1% while wage growth eased

• Signs of financial stress are picking up among renters

Employment increased by 90,400 in April, beating our expectations, following a modest decline in March. The gain was about split between full- and part-time employment. Despite the solid job growth during April, the unemployment rate was unchanged at 6.1%. Average hourly earnings for permanent workers moderated to 4.8%oya while the 3m/3m annualized gain moderated to 3.0% in April, the softest pace seen in some time (Figure 1). The easing in wage inflation and a steady unemployment rate alongside solid job growth continued to point to the supply-side improvement in the labor market. Looking through the monthly choppiness, underlying trend growth in the labor market—the employment gain on a six-month moving average basis—was 32,900 last month with all of the gain in service-sector jobs. The trend pace has been running at \~30,000 a month since last August, which is about equal to the trend pace through most of 2019. The evolution of the labor market continued to generally align with the Bank of Canada<sup>’</sup>s views in the April MPR. Job creation remains slower than the increase in the working-age population. The unemployment rate has risen gradually to 6.1% and the job vacancy rate has declined to a near pre-pandemic level. And lastly, there are signs that wage growth is easing. We are still looking for the first Bank of Canada 25bp policy rate cut in June. The next data report that is key to watch is the April CPI, the last CPI report before the Bank<sup>’</sup>s June announcement.

Figure 1: Average hourly earnings, permanent employees %, 3m/3m, saar, sa by J.P. Morgan
![](images/f45917c07a660b34d5d3cbe49cad8e114b639afb99018311f5ce8a2887f9e326.jpg)

The employment gain in April was led by service-producing industries and was a rebound story in many ways. Professional services, hospitality, and recreation employment all together gained 59,600 jobs in April following a 56,500 decline in those combined sectors in March. Goods employment fell

10.4K in April. In the last six months, goods sector hiring is down slightly on average. Total hours worked were up a robust 0.8% in April. The number of involuntary part-time workers was up sharply in April, our seasonal adjustment.

## Risks to, and the resilience of, the Canadian financial system

The Bank of Canada released its annual Financial Stability Report this week (renamed from Financial System Review). Canada<sup>’</sup>s financial system was found to be generally resilient. Households and businesses continue to adjust to higher interest rates although some indicators of financial stress look to be increasing. Moreover, higher debt-servicing costs will continue to touch mortgage holders over time and the reduced flexibility was judged to be a financial vulnerability in the event of a downturn. About half of all outstanding mortgages are held by borrowers who have yet to face higher rates because their payments were fixed for five years. However, solid income growth, accumulated savings, and reduced discretionary spending are buttressing households<sup>’</sup> ability to deal with higher debt payments. The report coincided with a survey of financial market participants. Survey respondents generally reported that they had a high degree of confidence in Canadian financial system resilience in the event of a severe economic shock, should one occur.

Many indicators of financial system stress have returned to more normal historical levels. On a more granular level, some signs of financial stress like loan delinquencies are growing and challenges are primarily concentrated among households without a mortgage, mainly renters according to consumer surveys. In contrast, indicators of financial stress among mortgage holders have remained low and stable. Facing a combination of higher inflation and higher interest rates, some households have increased their reliance on credit card debt. Research by Bank of Canada staff has found that households relying on credit cards to finance spending are more likely to experience financial stress in the future.

Figure 2: Delinquencies
![](images/6f6cac1fbc96e361f62a24fbfaa75552b1e0a930880e1d734df8f903f720e2eb.jpg)

## Mexico

• CPI eased but core services still trend higher

• Banxico recognized this in this week<sup>’</sup>s hawkish hold

• Strong upward revisions by CB to core CPI raise bar for June cut

• IP was strong as expected; composition was better than thought

Banxico left rates on hold as expected, and unanimously. The statement, however, was definitely hawkish, as Banxico threw in the towel on inflation reaching its target more rapidly; instead of 2H25, Banxico now expects inflation won<sup>’</sup>t reach the target until 4Q25. Also, revisions to this year<sup>’</sup>s inflation path were sizable, with headline inflation revised almost half a %-point higher in 4Q to 4%oya on average. Core revisions were more nuanced, but still sizable and of greater signal-value to us. Core inflation is seen at 3.8%oya in 4Q, still a touch below our forecast (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Quarterly inflation path - Core inflation
![](images/8b02a1ee6082ee4b623693578f79088ac913672248644439e20d0eba0851363f.jpg)

We think that the revisions to and delay of inflation convergence to target raise the bar for our call for Banxico to resume easing as soon as June, even if this remains our base-case. The minutes released two weeks from now will help us better gauge just how high the bar has gone. They should also shed light on how the central bank is factoring in a (for now) second-order variable, Fed policy.

Banxico<sup>’</sup>s focus was expected to be squarely on inflation and the statement did not disappoint. Nothing was said about the Fed in particular, whereas the reference to slow growth in 4Q23 and 1Q24 was fleeting, and tempered by a referral to continued “strength in the labor market.<sup>”</sup>

By contrast, Banxico seems to have done some soul-searching on CPI, and come to the conclusion that core services inflation will take much longer to come down meaningfully and that adverse weather conditions could amplify upside inflation risks (we<sup>’</sup>ve already seen the drought in many states of the country push raw food prices higher). To us, the latter part is secondary, but the acknowledgement that core services inflation will prove stickier in the statement (and not just mentioned as a risk in the minutes) was of first-order importance. We had been warning about services inflation, and did again earlier this week following what was an ugly print in non-tourism services (which remove Easter volatility).

Beyond the June decision, which will boil down to whether there is enough evidence of a change of trend in services inflation, the broader question is how much of the stickiness in services is seen as cyclical and how much as structural. To us, both have a say, as the strength of the consumer stems at least to a high degree to wage policy changes that have been in place since 2017, and are likely to remain in place for several years, at least.

Against this backdrop, even if the economy lost momentum, wage pressures could remain in place. It seems a reasonable assumption that the stickiness of core inflation back in late 2019, when the economy was in a recession, could have already been driven by this shift in policy. The answer to that question might be present in the minutes, and would impact our assessment of the terminal rate for the cycle (currently 7%), among other equally important questions on whether nominal neutral rates have risen in other key markets.

## A bit sweeter, but still bitter core CPI outlook

April CPI came a touch above market expectations, rising 0.2% in the month (nsa) and 4.65%oya from 4.42% in March. While the recent pickup in headline inflation could be frowned upon, the bulk of the increase came from rising raw food prices and, to a lesser degree, from a modest move up in gasoline prices. Fresh fruit and vegetable prices appear to continue to be under pressure from the drought affecting many states in the country. In fact, while our headline forecast was accurate, the composition was far from it, with core CPI in the second half of the month undershooting our forecast for a 0.18%2w/2w increase, rising 0.07% instead. Thus, core inflation continued to gradually trend lower, and was 0.2%-pt below its March level at 4.37%oya. Furthermore, in sequential terms, through some seasonal noise, core CPI gains have been decreasing and moving toward their historical norm: taking the March/April average, core prices increased 0.3%m m, sa.

That is welcome news, but it could prove unsustainable. The step down in monthly core CPI rates of change has been driven by goods, which benefited from weak domestic and global manufacturing, the abatement of supply chain issues, and an extremely strong currency. Smoothing out the noise related to Easter, services prices 3m3m annualized rate of change was steady at 5.4% in April. Furthermore, non-tourism services (services ex. housing and education, and tourism) price gains accelerated, rising at a 0.6%m/m, sa pace over the past six months; this metric had fallen to rates as low as 0.3% in the middle of last year. On a 3m3m basis it is running at 7.1% saar (Figure 2). This is the measure that in our view better reflects domestic inflation pressures and is the one Banxico is looking at more closely.

Figure 2: Core CPI - Non-tourism services and non-food goods %3m/3m, saar
![](images/c83176a13c5c709afcd210f88c4304b16f61b61289385e147107d5f3fd5442ec.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan with data from INEG

Against this backdrop, we acknowledge the aggregate improvement in core CPI trends (meaning disinflation), but are uncomfortable with its composition, which is not consistent with sustained core disinflation. For us to be more comfortable with our call for core CPI to converge to the upper bound of Banxico<sup>’</sup>s tolerance range we would need to see signs of sustainable services disinflation. Absent such signals, the risk is that as the disinflationary impulse benefiting goods prices starts to fade, core goods price increases will normalize to the upside, which, together with sticky services inflation, leads to a reacceleration in core price gains at some point.

## Manufacturing seems to be taking off

The flash 1Q GDP data already implied a roughly 0.7%m/m, sa gain in IP in March. Data were slightly softer than that at 0.6%m/m and there were some downside revisions to past data that left the February level 0.5%-0.6% below than what was earlier reported. However, today<sup>’</sup>s report was better than expected in a handful of ways: construction and manufacturing were revised higher; manufacturing grew more than expected; and, the gains in factory output are being more broad-based.

We have been growing gradually more comfortable with the idea that manufacturing might be finally out of the pothole it had been for more than a year, in line with what seems to be a common thread across many countries relevant to global manufacturing. The upside surprise in manufacturing (up 0.8%m/m vs. our 0.5% forecast) and the upward revision to past data (including February showing now a 0.7%m/m gain instead of 0.5%) was very welcome, and boosts our confidence.

However, it is encouraging that the current upswing in manufacturing could be more permanent than in the past, given that it is broad-based, and not just the auto sector. Our diffusion index continued to grind higher, and reached its highest level in more than two years. We noted this was likely going to be the case as indicated by the rise in intermediate goods imports in trade data for March, as the broader manufacturing sector is much more dependent on external inputs than the auto industry.

The big test will come in April data, starting with trade data to be published in about two weeks. After a big surge in past months<sup>’</sup> business surveys, PMIs generally pointed to a drop in output. However sentiment remained strong, capacity utilization high, and reported investment spiked. At the same time, following weakness in March, light-vehicles production surged 30%m/m, sa, although, admittedly, this has not been the most reliable indicator lately.

In less upbeat news, construction rose a bit less than we expected (1.1%m/m vs. 1.5%) and has moved sideways for some months now. So we remain unsure whether the sector has stagnated after surging for more than a year or if it is just taking a breather. By contrast mining production is undeniably weak and has slid further in the last handful of months, reflecting weakness in the oil and gas sector.

## Brazil

• COPOM reduced the easing pace in a split decision

• Risk of losing economic policy credibility has risen

• We raise 2024 CPI and fiscal deficit forecasts due to Rio Grande do Sul floods...

• ...but highlight the importance of COPOM<sup>’</sup>s minutes before fine-tuning policy rate and 2025 forecasts

The upbeat scenario whereby Brazil<sup>’</sup>s economy grows with low inflationary pressures this year and the next took another hit this week. The external outlook remains volatile, and the fears with public accounts dynamics are growing following the change in the 2025 primary target, but now also the risks of further losing policy credibility have risen with this week<sup>’</sup>s COPOM decision.

Following a change of tone in the rhetoric recently, the COPOM delivered a 25bp cut to 10.50% as expected, but both the decision and the more hawkish statement were outshined by the split score. Four deputy governors that were appointed by the current government voted for easing 50bp, while the remaining five members formed a majority for a 25bp cut. With the mandate of three out of the five COPOM members that voted for 25bp set to expire by the end of this year, the split decision spiked concerns on how the board will behave from 2025 onwards. The division was left unexplained in the statement. We expect that Tuesday<sup>’</sup>s minutes shine light on its extent and rationale.

Another challenge to this outlook is the shocking floods in the Rio Grande do Sul state (RS), which will leave the region with a large humanitarian and steep economic cost. Recordhigh rains left several thousands dislodged from their homes and destroyed critical infrastructure, and the reconstruction efforts will require federal funding, creating a dichotomy between expected results and those that will be checked against 2024<sup>’</sup>s fiscal target. The shock also is likely to raise inflation in the short run due to a shortage of products in RS, as well as beyond the next few months with the impact on key soy, rice, and corn production. Economic growth will also be disrupted and is likely to be negatively affected by 0.1%-0.3% of GDP mostly in 2Q.

The consequences of the floods thus put upward pressure on our CPI forecasts and downward pressure on GDP growth and fiscal results this year. On inflation, the impact of the floods is compounded by a previous rise in domestic commodity prices that will likely raise this year<sup>’</sup>s food CPI. We are thus moving our 2024 CPI forecast up two-tenths to 3.7%. We also expect the government to step up further the announcements of fiscal reconstruction aid to RS, whose

finances will be significantly hit. Assuming this, as well as some social aid transfers, we are also changing our call for a wider fiscal primary deficit of 0.8% of GDP, instead of the 0.5% previously estimated.

As for growth, our nowcasters are still running higher than our forecasts, with the 1Q nowcaster still 1%-pt above our call for a 2.4% expansion in 1Q GDP. In this sense, and considering that the extent of this crisis and the recovery effort in Rio Grande do Sul is likely large but still unclear, we will wait for the 1Q GDP release next month to fine tune our calls.

We are also not altering our monetary policy and 2025 calls though we recognize that the COPOM<sup>’</sup>s apparent inclination to take more risks on inflation in the coming years may drive up medium-to-long-term inflation expectations. If that happens, the risk is that the current board majority could even decide to halt the easing cycle already at the next meeting. But as we wait for next week<sup>’</sup>s meeting minutes we continue to see BCB cutting 25bp twice more to a terminal rate of 10%.

## April core CPI decelerated as expected

The April CPI came out at 0.38%m/m, nsa, just 3bp above consensus and matching our call. Core CPI also matched our expectations by rising 0.26%m/m, nsa. Headline and core CPI moved down further to 3.7% and 3.6%oya, respectively, approaching the 3% target. On top of that, core CPI decelerated to 2.8%3m/3m, saar, the first time below the target since the beginning of the pandemic (Figure 1), and core services decelerated to 3.9%m/m, saar—the first time within the target range in about six months—driving the 3-month average to 4.8%.

Figure 1: Seasonally adjusted core CPI
%, J.P. Morgan seasonal adjustment. Black lines show the target range
![](images/ef213dbf1fb2b945bf4df011f134805af99bfd34e8cd16fd47fb9d86ebe44555.jpg)

## Retail sales grew 10%q/q, saar in 1Q

After a strong start of the year, retail sales growth finally decelerated in March. Headline retail sales were down 0.3%m/m, sa in March, well below our call of 1.2% and consensus of 0.7%, but after two months in which they expanded 3.5% cumulatively. Core sales were stable, better than our

Fiscal sector

call for a 0.5% contraction and consensus at -0.3%. Despite this disappointing result, retail sales still expanded around 10%q/q, saar in 1Q, the strongest number since the beginning of 2023 (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Retail sales and private consumption
![](images/3256f6f684ef3190efeff9670c7a26fe3d33ca944e61b149febbcd0b1801cb90.jpg)
Source: IBGE and J.P. Morgan

## Inconsequential March primary surplus

The general government had a BRL1.2bn surplus in March, a sharp contrast with our BRL3.5bn and consensus BRL1.9bn deficit forecasts. The result pushed the 12m deficit to BRL252.9bn, equivalent to 2.3% of GDP and one-tenth lower than seen in February. Nominal results improved one-tenth to 9.1% of GDP, while gross and net public debt increased by two-tenths to 75.7% and 61.1% of GDP, respectively (Figure 3). While we welcome the positive surprise, we do not believe that it will change the more cautious or downright pessimistic stance that has prevailed in markets as of late.

## Argentina

• We estimate April CPI at 8.2%m/m, driving the overyear-ago print to 286.5%oya

• Core CPI expected to decelerate further to 5.8%m/m

• We now see year-end inflation at 155%oya amid the postponement of regulated price adjustments in 2Q

Real tax collection contracted again in April, down by 12.9%oya (10.1%oya YTD)

• Securing higher income sources remains paramount to consolidate the fiscal accounts

## Core CPI to consolidate in single-digits

We estimate April CPI at 8.2%m/m, driving the over-year-ago CPI print to 286.5%oya. Our headline inflation forecast (INDEC<sup>’</sup>s National CPI) is below the 8.8%m/m suggested by the high-frequency data set tracked by EcoGo through Aprilend. The monthly inflation print would thus continue its deceleration path amid the ongoing fiscal consolidation, the stable 2% monthly crawling-peg, and domestic demand contraction. April statistical carryover for May is estimated at 1.6%-pts, below the 3% and 4.5%-pts in the prior two months.

CABA April CPI came in at 9.8%m/m, with core CPI at 7.4%. Note that CABA CPI has printed on average 1.4%-pts above INDEC<sup>’</sup>S National headline CPI over the last four months. Inputting CABA variations into national CPI weights, the CABA print sets a floor to INDEC<sup>’</sup>s national CPI at 7.5%m/m.

Underlying monthly inflation is also poised to slow to singledigit levels. High-frequency data suggest core inflation will print at 5.8%m/m in April, decelerating further from 9.4%m/ m last month, and also below the 3mma pre-devaluation average of 12% (Sep-Nov 23). Meanwhile, our preferred metric of underlying inflation (adjusting core inflation by excluding food prices) is to ease to 6.1%m/m, from 9.2%m/m in March and the 14% monthly average increase reported in the prior three months.

We expect the inflation deceleration to prove widespread, with all categories but one easing on the month. Food inflation would spearhead the monthly deceleration, printing at 5.6%m/m in April, from a 14.3% monthly average in the prior three months, with the largest variations observed in fruits, and dairy products (+20.7% and 13.3%m/m , respectively). When looking at the weekly evolution, food inflation continues to decelerate to 1.0%w/w on average, or 4.1%m/m, returning to the levels seen in June 2023.

Meanwhile, furniture and household equipment, a category highly sensitive to the exchange rate and the economic cycle, plummeted to 2%m/m in our estimates, from 12.6% average in the prior three months, and the lowest level seen since 2018.

Regulated and seasonal prices still above the headline inflation level. We expect seasonal prices to post a +9%m/m variation, on the heels of +12%m/m average in the prior three months. Meanwhile, regulated prices would print again close to 20%m/m for the fifth month in a row amid the increases in gas and water tariffs, as well as fuels and pre-paid medical services on the month. The government announced it will postpone the indexation of gas and energy tariffs that were scheduled to start in May, with the objective to consolidate inflation further to the downside. Moreover, they have also postponed the adjustment of transportation tariffs for now and have mandated to backtrack pre-paid medical services increases in excess of cumulative inflation. While we are still waiting for further clarity on the pace of regulated prices adjustments ahead, we flag that this decision is expected to erase about 2.3%-pts from headline CPI in May vis-à-vis our prior forecast. That said, we still estimate close to 10%-pts on aggregate in repressed inflation ahead to realign relative prices to the levels seen by 2018.

## Against this backdrop, we see year-end inflation at

155%oya. Our prior base case assumed the front-loading of the bulk of the tariff adjustments in 1H24, but the above noted postponements push our 2Q24 inflation projections lower to an average of 6.5% on a monthly basis. In 2H24, inflation would average 5.5%m/m assuming (partial) utilities<sup>’</sup> tariff adjustments resume and the government starts relaxing capital controls by 3Q23 in a gradual manner (Figure 1). We now watch for April CPI release next week to fine-tune our inflation path if needed.

Figure 1: Headline CPI
![](images/37ae5d4b73408c477a46be6e71e4fa9e5dc18ef7694d580f73c705756867ece4.jpg)

## Real tax collection contracted again in April

Real tax collection contracted again in April on an annual basis, down by 12.9%oya (when deflating by last 12-m infla-

Week of May 13 - 17

tion) and -10.1%oya YTD. On a sequential basis, April<sup>’</sup>s real tax collection (J.P. Morgan<sup>’</sup>s seasonal adjustment) gained on the month, up by 1.2%m/m, sa, though coming on the heels of plunges of 3.7%m/m and 8.4%m/m, sa, respectively, reported in the previous two months. Thus, the three-month sequential momentum is now running at -3.4%3m/3m, saar. Of note, the last 3m average level (sa) remains at the lows since June 2020 when the COVID-19 lockdown was in place.

In the breakdown, the “PAIS tax<sup>”</sup> has continued to support tax collection YTD, increasing by 283%oya, and representing 7.2% of total tax collection in the first four months of the year, from just 1.7% in same period last year. On a sequential basis, the “PAIS tax<sup>”</sup> revenues contracted in April when adjusting for seasonality, down by 22.4%m/m, explained by the imports contraction on the back of the activity plunge and the slowdown in Bopreal issuance.

Meanwhile, foreign-trade tax collection increased by 47.1%oya in real terms YTD, explained by the end-2023 exchange rate devaluation. On a sequential basis, foreigntrade tax collection rebounded on the month (+10.4%m/m, sa), supported by higher export taxes on the back of the beginning of the soy harvest season.

Finally, when excluding foreign trade-related and PAIS tax collection, tax revenues declined 18.2%oya in real terms in April (18%oya YTD). On a sequential basis, the ex. foreigntrade tax revenues also gained on the month, +2.5%m/m, sa, interrupting two months of negative prints, though 3mma momentum remains in the red, at -6.7%3m/3m, saar.

Securing higher income sources remains paramount to consolidate the fiscal accounts. Ahead, the soy harvest should continue to support foreign trade-related tax collection in 2Q , but a rebound in sequential activity appears to be a necessary condition to sustain the fiscal consolidation over time. Securing higher income sources is also important for consolidating the fiscal accounts (Figure 2). Against this backdrop, after their approval in the Lower House, we now watch for the new Omnibus bill 2.0 and the fiscal package to be discussed in the Senate. The Upper House vote will likely take place the week of May 20. The voting outcome is expected to be closer than in the Lower House, but we still assign a high probability of the bill being approved in the Senate. That said, the risk is that the bill will see some modifications. From a fiscal perspective, the most relevant changes involve a tax amnesty as well as the re-incorporation of the income tax (both measures jointly accounting for about 0.8% of GDP in new revenues in our estimates this year). We keep our forecast for a fiscal primary balance this year, yet flag upside risks conditional on Omnibus bill 2.0 and fiscal package approval.

## Andeans

• Chile: April core CPI printed well above our estimate

2024 year-end inflation is projected at 4.0%oya, core at 4.3%oya (CBC’s central scenario at 3.8% for both)

• Colombia: April CPI logged 0.59%m/m (7.16%oya), roughly in line with our forecast

• Dec<sup>’</sup>24 headline CPI forecast is maintained at 5.2%oya

We also keep in our base case BanRep shaving the policy rate by another 50bp in June, to 11.25%

## Chile: Spotlight on core persistence

April headline inflation printed a bit above expectations, but core CPI was well above our estimate. As discussed below, the sequential adjustment shows both headline and particularly core prices experiencing strong persistence, a factor the central bank should weigh in when assessing the pace of easing as well as the terminal rate for the current cycle.

The details show that headline CPI logged 0.53%m/m in April (JPMe: 0.5%; BBG consensus: 0.4%). Thus, the last 12-m inflation (referential CBC splice) converged to 3.5%oya, from 3.2% by February.

When adjusting for seasonality (J.P. Morgan sa adjustment), headline CPI logged 0.5%m/m, sa, basically aligning with the monthly average observed in the first three months of the year. The last 3-month annualized momentum converged to 5.6 %ar, which compares to 2.8% for the period ending in Dec<sup>’</sup>23 (Figure 1).

![](images/73f363f682b34c745a8b8fd285557c1de96beecae83abeb2342eff94f1d24b73.jpg)
Source: INE, CBC and J.P. Morgan

Moving to underlying inflation, ex. volatile CPI recorded 0.50 % on the month, with the annual rate easing by a whisker to 3.5%oya, from 3.6% by March. When adjusting for seasonality (J.P. Morgan sa adjustment), core logged %0.4m/m, sa, also in line with the YTD monthly average. The last 3-m annualized pace recorded a still high 4.9%ar.

In all, in sequential terms both headline and core CPI are not only showing persistence, but accelerating. Admittedly, the exchange rate has played a role, particularly in terms of gasoline prices (up by 5.2%YTD). But more relevant, in our view, is underlying core services pressures. In terms of forecasts, the 2024 year-end headline CPI is projected at 4.0%oya, while core CPI at 4.3%oya (CBC<sup>’</sup>s central scenario 3.8% for both headline and core CPI).

## Colombia: Inflation aligns to expectations

There were no surprises on the inflation front. The Apr<sup>’</sup>24 monthly CPI printed at 0.59%m/m, roughly in line with our forecast for 0.58%, driving annual inflation lower to 7.17%oya, from 7.36% in the prior month.

Adjusting the time series for seasonality, a best gauge for the current inflation momentum, headline CPI logged 0.45%m/m, sa. The monthly print recorded a bit above the prior 3-month average of 0.51%. Thus, the 3-month annualized pace, a smoother metric to gauge momentum, accelerated to 6.39%, still well above the target range ceiling. The gap between the last 12-month and the 3m, saar rates aligns with the base case for a sustained downward trend for the annual CPI ahead, though to still-above-policy-range levels over the year.

Regarding underlying inflation, core CPI as tracked by Ban-Rep (ex-food & regulated) gained by 0.52%m/m, again decelerating materially when compared to the same month last year when it logged 0.9%. In the same vein as for headline inflation, on an annual basis core decelerated to 6.4%oya, from 6.8% in March. As relevant, the seasonal adjustment showed a monthly pace of 0.37 %m/m, sa, with the last 3m, saar pace down by a tad, to 4.9%ar. We continue to stress that a more detailed breakdown of the core CPI dynamics shows the differential behavior of goods and services (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Colombia CPI
![](images/386168f43e7d5b3156c502da337b434dbbc7899b2d739c4103e1f5617896d182.jpg)

In terms of the CPI forecast, we maintain as our base case headline CPI converging to 5.2 %oya by year-end, a bit below BanRep<sup>’</sup>s central scenario (see note). On monetary policy, our base case is for a new 50bp policy rate cut at the upcoming

meeting scheduled for the end of June. Admittedly, downside surprises to May CPI, or a relaxation of global financial conditions may open the door to accelerating the easing cycle before what we have penciled in our base case. Note that we do expect an acceleration of the easing cycle in 2H24, assuming the Fed starts to adjust the policy rate lower. We project the policy rate level for Dec-24 at 8.5%. On the terminal rate, we maintain the level at 6.25%, due to a higher r\* than what BanRep estimates due to fiscal risk (see note for discussion).

## United Kingdom

• BoE turns dovish despite stronger data

• Raising the odds of a June cut

• Growth is already far exceeding BoE forecasts, however, with more data to come

• We stick with Aug for now, but the call is very close

The BoE left rates unchanged at its latest meeting, with an extra dovish dissent from Ramsden taking the vote to 7-2 as generally expected. But the statement contained a new passage that leans in a more dovish direction, noting that the MPC “will consider forthcoming data releases and how these inform the assessment that the risks from inflation persistence are receding<sup>”</sup>. This was inserted just before unchanged guidance stating the MPC would “keep under review for how long Bank Rate should be maintained at its current level.<sup>”</sup>

On the surface the language change noted above is subtle with plenty of conditionality, but the rhetoric behind it appears designed to more clearly open the door to a potential June cut. For example, its not at all clear that inflation persistence is actually receding. But the MPC judges that the risks associated with that persistence are, and now believes that second round effects will fade more quickly than before. Reading further between the lines, the new guidance could be interpreted as the MPC lining up a cut unless the key data prior to the June meeting do something to change their new assessment, i.e. by surprising to the upside of its expectations. This is different to a situation where downside surprises are necessary to prompt an early cut. This lowers the bar for June.

If this reading is correct then the timing of such a shift looks odd, as the main indicators the BoE has focussed on have surprised it to the upside since February. Given issues with the labour market and pay data at the moment, there is a greater focus on the services CPI in particular. This metric surprised the BoE by a significant +0.2%-pts in March. Bailey in the opening remarks of the press conference conceded that this “should certainly give us pause for thought<sup>”</sup>. But he followed up by dismissing the surprise as a by-product of the “ups and downs in the data<sup>”</sup> and “within the volatility we should expect in normal times<sup>”</sup>. After laying out a clear framework and arguing that it is data dependent, it feels like the MPC has sought to step around this and shift its tone anyway.

After placing a prominent weight on a persistence weighted “supercore<sup>”</sup> measure of services inflation, there is now no mention of this in the minutes despite the measure in question running at a 5-6% annualized rate. The BoE instead highlights the decline in some survey based measures of pricing.

The MPC<sup>’</sup>s forecasts assume that the March CPI services inflation surprise will persist in April and May. That further raises the bar for this key data metric to surprise it to the upside before June. Another significant signal came from the BoE<sup>’</sup>s medium term CPI forecast based on market rates, which is shown below target at 1.9% at the key two year horizon. This shortfall was also referenced during the statement, as if to highlight the importance of the signal.

The BoE also went further in talking about the future rate path in general. Inflation at the three year horizon was shown down at 1.6% based on market rates, a clear sign that it believes market rate pricing over the next year or so is too high. We had expected it to hint in this direction. But the message was made more explicit. After spending much of the past three years arguing that it would not comment on broader market pricing, Bailey<sup>’</sup>s opening remarks stated that “it is likely that we will need to cut Bank Rate over the coming quarters and make monetary policy somewhat less restrictive over the forecast period, possibly more so than currently priced in market rate<sup>”</sup>.

With four members having pushed back on the idea of a very near term easing lately, the impression we are left with is that Bailey and Broadbent have shifted their views. Bailey caveated this tilt in his opening remarks by noting that “Before our next meeting in June, we will have two full sets of data – for inflation, activity and the labour market – that will help us in making that judgement afresh. But, let me be clear, a change in Bank Rate in June is neither ruled out nor a fait accompli<sup>”</sup>. A spread of views among non-dissenting members is still clearly evident in the minutes, and a potential June cut looks like it would be a very close vote. But the centre of the MPC does appear to have shifted somewhat independently of data surprises.

We are leaving our call for an August cut unchanged for now given there is a lot of data still to come. But we will be watching a few indicators in evaluating the risk of June. These include the next CPI report in two week<sup>’</sup>s time. We currently forecast a 5.3-5.4% for April services inflation, versus the BoE<sup>’</sup>s latest 5.5% forecast. April is a key month given some services firms reset their prices infrequently, with a tendency to do so at the start of the financial year. Risks of a surprise may therefore be higher for this reason and so its is an important release. After making no mention of its “supercore<sup>”</sup> services inflation measure in the minutes, the BoE instead went on to highlight indicators of pricing power within its own Agents survey as well as pricing intentions among consumer-facing services firms in its DMP survey (next due on 6<sup>th</sup> June). The BoE also mentions April pay settlements data, but the next average earnings data will also be important after the prior surprise.

## 1Q GDP beats expectations

GDP for March exceeded all expectations with a strong 0.4%m/m gain and a one tenth upward revision taking the prior month to 0.2%. That left GDP for the quarter as a whole posting a 0.6%q/q gain (JPMorgan, Consensus, BoE 0.4%), equivalent to a 2.5% annualized pace. While the data are volatile, this more than offsets the GDP declines reported for 2H23.Despite the surprise in 1Q GDP, consumption still came in on the low side of expectations with a 0.2%q/q gain, with investment instead providing a greater lift of 1.4%q/q. Business investment rose 0.9%, which is not bad by UK standards. If household real income gains stay strong as we expect, that could imply there is more to come from the consumer this quarter. The inventory and trade data are less reliable, but provided offsetting moves.

For a while now we have been optimistic on growth in the early part of this year. But the 1Q data have exceeded those expectations and positive momentum at the end of the quarter challenges our 2Q forecast as well. The PMI is running around 54 and signals that solid momentum has continued into the spring. We are revising up 2Q GDP from 0.4% to 0.5%q/q (or from 1.5% to 2.0% annualized). This again brings into question the BoE<sup>’</sup>s latest projection for growth to slow to 0.2%q/q in 2Q (0.8% annualized). Strong positive upside surprises to the BoE<sup>’</sup>s growth forecasts should in theory reduce some of the urgency for a cut next month. But the BoE this week set aside most of the stronger incoming data to pivot in a more dovish direction. It is therefore hard to know exactly where the thresholds are at the moment to gauge the likelihood of a June vs. August cut.

Signs that labour demand is firming in response to stronger GDP would be one relevant factor. Its hard to tell yet whether that is happening. Several jobs indicators have been weak, although with surveys recently showing a clearer improvement (see below).

## REC weak with hints of recovery

Faith in the REC jobs survey has been challenged since the pandemic as several of its once reliable relationships with the official labour market data have broken down. We are hence not inclined to place a lot of weight on its monthly moves at present. Nevertheless, it is worth mentioning that the permanent placements reading in April jumped by just over three points to 46.4, the highest in a year. That is still low compared to the PMI, but leaves both surveys showing a trend higher since late last year. Staff availability continued to rise, suggesting no obvious resource constraints, but the permanent salaries reading did show a rare rise of 2.6-pts to 55.9. While still very low, this reverses all of the drop in the prior month and indeed the declines seen since February.

With GDP growth now apparently recovering, an important question is whether this demand uplift is absorbed by better productivity or instead generates higher labour demand that creates renewed wage pressures in the labour market. It is too early to answer that question, but the directional change in the April REC survey (and the latest jump higher in average hours worked in the official data) do appear to highlight at least some risk of the latter.

We look for a 0.3%m/m, sa gain in private sector regular pay in the March data, which would produce a step down in the annual growth rate to 5.8%, assuming no revisions. The big question is whether this would exceed the BoE<sup>’</sup>s forecasts. The data they released this week, however, appears to show two different forecasts for the same set of data which make it unclear what would constitute a surprise. For 1Q, there is one forecast showing private sector regular pay at 5.6% and another at 6.0%. The suspicion of many observers is that the BoE has set the bar high for an upside surprise, and so we would assume the latter is probably the one it wanted to publish. But it does beg the question of where the lower forecast originally came from. We also expect the weak signal from the recent jobs data to fade, with revisions or latest outturns showing smaller declines.
