## Emerging Europe

• Romania: We see a 25bp cut, but it is a coin toss

Israel: Fiscal deficit up to 7% of GDP on 12m-trailing basis

• Poland: NBP keeps rates unchanged, as expected

• CEE: retail data confirms return of the consumer

## Romania: NBR might start easing cycle

National Bank of Romania (NBR) finds itself in a bit of conundrum. In February, Governor Isarescu gave clear forward guidance that rate cuts are coming based on some conditions that were met since: two months of disinflation were recorded and actual inflation, both core and headline, was close to NBR<sup>’</sup>s forecast for 1Q24. Note that Governor Isarescu and NBR post-meeting statement, usually do not have any forward guidance on purpose – this way the bank has more freedom in its decisions. Thus, the guidance of cuts was highly unusual and very surprising.

However, global conditions have changed – less because of the ECB, more because of the Fed and US inflation. More important, domestic inflation prints, while in line with NBR<sup>’</sup>s forecast, had quite poor details as core goods inflation is around 8% and core services around 10%. We stated post March CPI that rate cuts in such an environment are not justified. And NBR sent a similar message as minutes released for the April meeting had removed a para present in February minutes, a para which explained conditions for rate cuts cycle to start soon.

We keep the call for 25bp rate cut on guidance from the NBR governor but see big chances of unchanged rate. The meeting on Monday is a coin toss because it really depends on whether the governor will decide to keep his promise. Based on the data, no rate cut is possible. To make it even harder, the NBR inflation forecast was too optimistic and an upward revision towards our forecast is likely. Thus, cutting the key rate when both realized and projected inflation would not support it, will not be easy. With elevated inflation likely to stay, NBR has little room to cut this year - we have 75bp in total, including next week.

## Israel: Fiscal deficit widens

The budget posted a deficit of ILS11.7bn in April, which brought the 12m-trailing deficit up to 7.0% of GDP from 6.2% previously. Some re-acceleration in deficit widening was apparently led by delays in tax collection amid shifted holiday schedule — due to Passover some tax payments were delayed from April to May. Judging by available data, VAT and customs payments were likely most affected by this

delay, as they were down 38%oya in April. Without this effect, the 12m-trailing deficit would be 6.7% instead of 7.0%.

Figure 1: Budget revenues and expenditures - Israel
![](images/57fba9f7e06fd0ca41401ad5c6dc2959cb441811395e45584541ed41e7ee67d7.jpg)
Source: MoF, Haver, J.P. Morgan

The main incremental news from the report was that defense spending dropped to around ILS10bn last month, from the more typical 12-17bn in the prior five months — declining intensity of fighting in Gaza apparently helped the performance. Overall expenditures rose 35.8%oya in January-April, while, excluding war-related spending, expenditures were up (a still elevated) 11.7%.

Our forecast has been for the budget deficit to reach 6.3% of GDP in 2024, which assumes some improvement in budget performance in the second half of the year, both in revenues (economic recovery) and expenditures (reduced war spending). The recent monthly budget reports are not out of line with our assumptions, however, for our forecast to get realized, it will be crucial that the intensity of war spending declines into 2H24. Recent developments around Rafah suggest that risks to our deficit forecast may be skewed to upside.

Figure 2: Main expenditures - Israel
![](images/7dbc4fde0fd9a0d832f1e73ba03daa46e5c0568747a94d6a237dc52e3b628bc1.jpg)

Source: MoF, Haver, J.P. Morgan

In the meantime, the Finance Ministry began preparations for the 2025 budget and reportedly is looking for ways to improve the fiscal accounts by an additional ILS30bn next year as an extra 20-30bn is demanded by the Defence Ministry. For that, an additional VAT hike (on top of already approved 1%-pt increase to 18%), skipped indexation of income tax brackets, improved tax administration and a few

Czech Republic:

other revenue-raising measures are considered. As we are in relatively early stages of the 2025 budget cycle, we consider press reports on the matter as part of the natural horse trading process and would wait to see the outcome of spending and revenue discussions closer to the end of the year to draw conclusions.

## Poland: NBP keeps rates unchanged

As universally expected, the NBP kept the policy rate unchanged at 5.75% (Figure 3) for the seventh consecutive meeting. The message in the statement was essentially the same as before, explaining that inflation has slowed significantly as supply disruptions abated and cost pressures were reduced, growth is improving but still subdued, and this, together with strong Zloty contributes to keeping CPI at levels consistent with the target. However, the text still argues, some actions by the authorities pose upside risks to inflation developments. The first upside risk is the upcoming changes to regulated energy prices mid-year , and the second is a potential demand acceleration due to higher wage growth (“stemming i.a. from wage increases in the public sector<sup>”</sup>). All in all, the MPC considers it is better to keep rates unchanged. Until the uncertainty disappears, that is, until we get the July/August CPI numbers revealing the extent of the impact from energy prices the message is likely to repeat the same notes every month. We expect two small rates cuts of 25bps each in November and December.

![](images/846087e703854ff33b659e39ad79d65b43ee727c21ae37313c05259463551500.jpg)

## CEE: Return of the consumer

March retail sales data published this week for the Czech Republic and Hungary corroborated our thesis of a turnaround in consumer spending becoming the key pillar of growth in 2024. Retail sale volumes were up 1.3%m/m sa in the Czech Republic, which brings the quarter as a whole to 1.9%q/q, an 8% annualized pace (Figure 4). In Hungary we saw similar dynamics (Figure 5): sales volumes increased 2%m/m sa in March, leading to a quarterly gain of 1.9%q/q, also near 8% annualized (7.9% more concretely). Encouragingly, in both cases sales were lifted by all sub-categories,

including non-essentials, which suggests a more robust consumer picture. The turn in the consumer is driven mainly by a rebound in real incomes, as inflation fell sharply to low single digits, whereas wage growth has been stickier, generating dynamics of large real wage gains. Even as we expect wage growth to slow, that is set to be much more gradual than the decline of inflation, and real incomes are likely to support consumer spending throughout 2025.

## South Africa

• Voter-adjusted estimates put ANC support at 44%

• Weak manufacturing output in 1Q24

• Curbs on energy supply ease; EAF improving

Opinion polls continue to trickle in with a consistent message of ANC support below 50%. The now-released voter turnoutadjusted estimate of IPSOS puts ANC support at 44% in the medium turnout scenario and 43% in the high turnout scenario. MK support is seen at 8.7% with the EFF at 11% and the DA at 20.9%, leaving the IFP at 3.3% (medium turnout). The IPSOS estimated an uplift to the ANC from the less than 100% turnout of 3.2%, in line with what we expected IPSOS to show. That said, in the 2019 election the turnout adjustment (and perhaps underlying sample variation) led to a notable over-projection of ANC support. Moreover, it is not clear whether the tendency of ANC supporters to more likely participate on election day (vs. esp. minor parties) is as notable now compared to prior elections. Our base case remains one of an ANC gaining close enough to 45% of voter support to enable a coalition with minor opposition parties, such as the IFP, PA, and GOOD, to bring it to the 50% mark.

## Downside 1Q24 GDP growth bias

The manufacturing output lift we had predicted to sustain IP did not fully materialize in 1Q. In 4Q23 the sector failed to recover from the 4.9%q/q saar slump in 3Q with a meagre 0.8% rise, and we had anticipated a further catchup last quarter. Instead, we now estimate manufacturing production to have plunged 3.7%q/q saar in 1Q after an unexpected 2.2% nosedive in March. The petroleum, metals and vehicle manufactures recorded the largest output losses. A host of factors explain the slump, including a shutdown of two plants by a key automobile manufacturer (see here). Next week<sup>’</sup>s mining production and retail sales data should help us fine tune our full quarter GDP estimate (0.4%q/q currently).

Figure 1: Maximum installed rooftop solar
![](images/9b245cecc58fa847504d47178c9bb60c152436e6d50ea7be5cb47253ecc1c12c.jpg)
Source: Eskom, no data for May and June 2022 so we asssume flat from Apri

Looking ahead, the potential to supply should benefit from incremental gains witnessed in the energy space. Load-shedding intensity has reduced compared to 2023, and the country experienced no power cuts in April, which should support 2Q GDP. While it is easy to view this in the context of the upcoming elections, the private sector response with alternative energy sources is helping reduce demand on the national grid (Figure 1). In addition, energy availability has improved in recent weeks on reduced plants breakdown, thanks to cooler weather supporting coal plant performance (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Weekly energy availability factor (EAF)
![](images/c8b1885e8484547597e188947d17b017cc6d47da9f39a306b065129f39ae9791.jpg)

## Australia and New Zealand

The RBA kept the cash rate on hold and left guidance largely unchanged at the May meeting ...

… while revising up their near-term inflation forecast and revising down the unemployment rate forecast

• We think Australian wages grew 0.9%q/q in 1Q24

• We expect Australia<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate rose one tenth to 3.9% in April

The RBA kept the cash rate steady as widely expected at 4.35%, and also maintained its relatively open-ended guidance that the "the path of interest rates that will best ensure that inflation returns to target in a reasonable time frame remains uncertain and the Board is not ruling anything in or out".

In the board<sup>’</sup>s statement, inflation was deemed to be “easing<sup>”</sup>, but “doing so more slowly than previously expected<sup>”</sup>. Reflecting this, the inflation forecasts were revised up substantially for 2024, with the Dec-24 projection for headline up 0.6%-pt to 3.8%oya, and trimmed mean raised by 0.3%-pt to 3.4%. Inflation still is assumed to return to the target band in 2025.

The concluding paragraph conveys some sense of growing impatience, as the board is now "vigilant to upside risks", which harks back to the "limited further tolerance" tone leading up to the last rate hike in November. Still, on net the magnitude of inflation forecast upgrade and the lack of policy response, convey a more dovish reaction function than the market assumed heading into the meeting (i.e., post CPI).

The upward revision to the staff's inflation forecast mostly reflects a boost from local fuel prices (near-term) and a change in assumption around services inflation, which is expected to remain elevated for longer. The wage forecasts underpinning this haven't changed materially, nor has the unemployment path. Moreover, recent productivity outcomes have been strong and the forecast does not assume any payback on that this year. The implication is that margins will add to inflation more than expected. Given the recent breakdown of the CPI data, it is reasonable to attribute this to sectors like insurance, health, and rents.

In the press conference, Governor Bullock argued “rates are at the right level<sup>”</sup> to achieve the target, but “we cannot let high inflation take hold<sup>”</sup> and nominated inflation expectations as a key channel to watch, in guiding the board<sup>’</sup>s tolerance. In terms of the more conventional indicators, the magnitude of inflation forecast upgrades this week means the hurdle is high to register a further upside surprise in coming CPI reports. If another hike is to come, the trigger is more likely to come from the growth and/or employment channels, particularly since weakness in per-capita spending is currently anchoring the board<sup>’</sup>s view that policy is restrictive. We expect real consumption growth to pick up over the next few quarters, but only in moderate fashion.

## Retail volumes create downside risk

Australian retail volumes were softer than forecast, contracting 0.4%q/q in the March quarter (consensus -0.3%q/q; JPM: 0.3%q/q). This is a direct input into our GDP tracking framework and imparts downside risk to our standing household consumption forecast for a modest gain of 0.3%q/q. With that in mind, the import data were very strong early in the year so it<sup>’</sup>s likely the weakness is at least partly offset by a stronger inventory build.

Figure 1: Australian retail volumes
![](images/92f502091be98bb3ec8d403b06976245532ac24cc4d4cb222882c7a1d4f6c5f0.jpg)

In terms of the detail, the weakness in this week<sup>’</sup>s print was narrowly focused in household goods where real turnover collapsed 3%q/q. The remainder of the groups were more upbeat with clothing (+1.3q/q), cafes/restaurants (+0.3%q/q) and <sup>‘</sup>other<sup>’</sup> retailing (+0.5%q/q) posting small gains, while department stores slipped 0.4%q/q.

## Australian U-rate grinding higher

We think Australia<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate rose one tenth to 3.9% in April, alongside a steady participation rate (66.6%) and a 10K increase in employment. The jobless rate rose 0.1%-pt in the March release, confirming that the very strong February print represented unwind from seasonal weakness around the turn of the year. The broader details were strong in March too, with hours work climbing close to 1%m/m and the underemployment rate declining a tenth to a low 6.5% (Figure 2).

Week of May 13 - 17

Figure 2: Australia underemployment and unemployment rates % of labour force, both axes
![](images/df3743b12e4620856a17eb636bb01baae9c9a42778392d7a7258cbe2d7301f5f.jpg)

The usual relationship between domestic demand and the labor market implies the unemployment rate should move higher over 2024, but we expect this process to be gradual. The labor market remains tight in aggregate, and the probability of an individual moving from employment to unemployment (the separation rate) has remained very low and stable. The low separation rate implies labor demand remains strong, as firms are unwilling to reduce existing headcount.

## Wage growth remaining steady in 1Q

Wage growth is expected to print at 0.9%q/q in 1Q24, consistent with the prior quarter<sup>’</sup>s outcome and enough to see the annual rate hold at 4.2%oya. The unemployment rate (3.8%) remain low versus the historical average, but has increased from the 2022 low and suggests that the impulse from labor demand to private wages set by individual arrangements is easing. In contrast, wage growth remains strong for enterprising bargaining agreements (EBA) where the flow of recently renewed private and public contracts are setting above aggregate WCI growth. The resilience of wages relative to inflation means real wage growth will strengthen in coming quarters, a dynamic which we expect will benefit household consumption later in the year.

Figure 3: Australia wage price index
![](images/78ae5099521654c80fcbf76741897852ff45ccea0925d637b1f033d4e11ca32f.jpg)

## Greater China

• China: April exports on a solid sequential growth trend, imports beat expectations

• Larger-than-expected \$45bn fall in FX reserves

Taiwan: CPI inflation eased to 1.95%oya despite electricity price hike, reducing risk of a June rate hike

• Exports moderated, U.S. demand strength continued

• Next week: China inflation, credit, FAI, IP, retail sales and home prices

Following the general strong growth pace in 1Q24, the solid momentum of China<sup>’</sup>s export activity carried into April. Merchandise exports (in USD terms) rose 1.5%oya or 2.8% m/m, sa (Figure 1). By product breakdown, there were broad-based gains across major product groups, including low-end consumer goods (+0.6% m/m sa), mechanical and electrical products (+4.3%) and high-end products (+2.4%). Auto exports fell a modest 1.4% m/m sa, following the 18.4% jump in March. By major destination, exports to the DM markets posted decent gains, including the U.S. (+5.1% m/m sa) and Europe (4.0%). Exports to EM Asia (in particular, ASEAN), LatAm and Africa also sustained a solid growth trend.

Figure 1: China merchandise trade growth
![](images/34280a0c735b75ae3322cd3f6b95bb3feca91316861b4b55a23a10d297a8f92f.jpg)
Source: China Customs, J.P. Morgan

Meanwhile, nominal imports came in above expectations, rising 2.5% m/m sa or 3.4% 3m/3m saar. Imports from the U.S. (+15.1% m/m sa) and Europe (+5.1%) outperformed, along with solid import growth from EM Asia (+6.2%). Imports of mechanical and electrical products (+2.7%) and high-tech products (+3.9%) were solid, as well, along with a mixed trend in imports of commodities. As a result, the trade surplus came in at US\$72.4bn in April, compared to \$58.6 billion in March.

China<sup>’</sup>s export activity has held up well in recent quarters, benefiting from diversification across major markets (EM Asia, LatAm, Russia and Africa) and by product (with rapid expansion in new energy products and motor vehicles). We expect relatively steady growth trends in China<sup>’</sup>s export activity through 2024 amid a constructive global growth outlook. Following solid manufacturing FAI growth and capacity expansion in the past two years, and amid lingering softness in domestic demand conditions, Chinese exporters will continue to push to expand their global market share. Trade partners<sup>’</sup> potential reactions will become a growing concern this year, however.

## FX reserves dropped US\$45bn

April FX reserves missed market expectations. Reserves declined US\$44.8bn, to US\$3,200.8bn, the largest monthly fall in more than half a year. Under our latest forecast, we look for the current account surplus to inch up to US\$24.7bn in April from US\$20.1bn on average in 1Q. We note that the U.S. dollar index moved up from 104.5 to 106.2, leading to an estimated currency valuation loss of US\$22.6bn. With that, we tentatively estimate a US\$47.0bn implied capital outflow for April, after inflows of \$6.8bn in February and \$10.5bn in March.

While we did look for a return of capital outflow for April, the implied estimates from the FX reserves change are larger than expected. We think this is due partly to valuation effects related to China<sup>’</sup>s holding of different financial assets, such as U.S. rate products, etc. (Figure 2). The 10-year U.S. treasury yield moved up nearly 50bps, from 4.2% to 4.69%, as the market was repricing Fed rate expectations.

Figure 2: China's capital outflow and 10-year UST yield
![](images/4a8bcd1f59c76fccdb6f71426a5d4e020de3f2b76238bbfb5338df96fb28de6b.jpg)
Source: SAFE, Federal Reserve, J.P. Morgan

With “higher for longer<sup>”</sup> gaining more ground, CNY depreciation pressure will continue and will likely last for a longerthan-anticipated period of time. Despite a marginally higher CNY fixing, a touch above 7.10 since mid-April, the gap between CNY fixing and the spot price from the previous trading day remains elevated. Technically, the CNY basket, CFETS TWI index, has largely followed the dollar move, with appreciation against most other major currencies in recent months. For example, CNY appreciation against JPY and KRW explains 54% of the CFETS gain in April. If the PBOC maintains a strong CNY fixing bias, CFETS may stay

Figure 4: Taiwan export breakdown by products - level US\$ bn, sa, 3mma

relatively elevated around current levels before trending down into year-end following the anticipated fading of dollar strength.

## Taiwan: CPI inflation eased to 1.95%

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s CPI inflation delivered a downside surprise for the second consecutive month in April, as headline CPI eased further, to 1.95%oya from March<sup>’</sup>s 2.1% (Figure 3), staying flat in %m/m terms. The further easing in the April CPI is surprising, considering the electricity price hike, which led to a 4.5% m/m nsa rise in the electricity fee component. Food prices rose 2.6%oya in April, staying unchanged in %m/m sa terms. Core CPI inflation eased to 1.8%oya in April (vs. 2.1%oya in March), also flat in %m/m sa terms.

Figure 3: Taiwan headline and core CPI
![](images/704a99a8e9f26a362d0d41ebe46f2a5bcedc9fa161c08878ffa6f109e76d10bf.jpg)
2011 2012 2013 2014 2015 2016 2017 2018 2019 2020 2021 2022 2023 2024 Source: DGBAS, J..P. Morgan

Putting aside LNY-related volatility, it appears that the underlying inflation trend has been steadily easing. Headline CPI rose by an average 2.24%oya in the first four months this year, compared to 2.9% on average in 4Q23. Looking ahead, the easing in employment growth (and household income) will likely restrain the pace of domestic demand growth, and hence core CPI inflation further down the road. Assuming some moderate indirect effect from the electricity price hike in the coming months, we expect Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s headline CPI inflation to stay around 2% during May-July before easing meaningfully below 2% toward 2H.

On monetary policy, the Taiwanese central bank announced a surprising hike of 12.5bp in March, reflecting its intension to preemptively contain inflation expectations, especially considering the (direct and indirect) impact of the April electricity price hike. The downside surprises in the April CPI report despite the electricity price hike, and the general easing in the underlying inflation trends, are encouraging, helping to ease concerns that CPI inflation may stay higher than expected for a sustained period of time. Our baseline scenario looks for the central bank to keep policy rates on hold for the rest of 2024, and we see a low probability of another modest policy rate hike in June.

## Exports moderated in April

Following the solid sequential growth trend in 4Q23 and 1Q24, Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s April trade report showed some moderation in export activity, although AI-related tech demand and shipments to the U.S. continued to show solid growth momentum. April merchandise exports came in below expectations, rising 4.3%oya, following significant upside surprise in March (+18.9%oya). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that exports (in volume terms) fell 5.7% m/m sa in April after the 13.9% strong increase in March.

In the breakdown, tech exports fell 3.8% m/m sa after March<sup>’</sup>s 9.1% jump, with the sequential trend rising 12.1% 3m/3m saar (Figure 4). There was a strong underlying expansion trend in the exports of information and communication products (+137.1% 3m/3m saar), reflecting strong growth in the exports of AI-related products. Non-tech exports declined 7.3% m/m sa or 14.5% 3m/3m saar. Machinery exports softened, with a 2.9% m/m sa fall. Breakdown by export destination highlights ongoing outperformance in exports to the U.S. (+76.5% 3m/3m saar), along with solid growth in exports to the ASEAN region (+31.7%), while exports to China/HK SAR continue to underperform (-23.9%).

![](images/483f5cf8e177c2f8cb1dae1c1b9d69f82eb87cf9aad1c58937ec55bf699de9af.jpg)

We look for a steady upward trend in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s tech exports in the coming quarters, including a structural boost of AI-related products and HPC-related demand, while the tech sector<sup>’</sup>s destocking pressure begins to ease. The recent sequential upturn in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s capital goods imports is encouraging, suggesting a tentative recovery in the domestic capex trend, likely reflecting Taiwan manufacturers<sup>’</sup> improving sentiment on the export sector outlook.

## Korea

• Current account to recover further after April dip..

• … with TOT tailwinds for goods trade surplus

• Both in and outbound tourist flows are rising sharply

The seasonally-adjusted current account surplus dipped in April, falling from a 2.5-year high US\$7.7bn in February to US\$6.4bn in March. Zooming out, it has steadily risen to US\$6.9bn/month in the first three months of this year, from US\$1.4bn in 1H23 and US\$4.5bn in 2H23, led by a recovery in the merchandise trade surplus. The trade surplus has recovered since it ended a streak of deficits in April 2023 and marked a three-year high US\$8.8bn, sa, this January, followed by slightly narrower US\$8.2bn in February and US\$7.4bn in March. We continue to expect that the trade surplus should broadly continue the recovery trend, as its key supporting factors—the ongoing upturn of tech exports and stabilization in oil import prices—remain intact (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Korea goods trade balance
![](images/642ada1d59e619fe7d6399539db0d485bc52cfc26b91e532e7169ded04bd27c7.jpg)

In the latest April customs data, tech exports marked 20.8%3m/3m, saar, growth, remaining resilient even after deceleration from a 65.0% peak in December. Customs imports rose 4.2%m/m in April, starting to reflect the rise in global oil prices, and such pass-through may continue for a few more months due to a time lag. That said, the customs trade surplus was only partially pared in April, still surpassing last year<sup>’</sup>s highs, and global oil prices recently moderated, aligning with our outlook for stabilization (Brent \$90/bbl through May and \$85 in 2H24) which has been maintained despite geopolitical risks.

## Invisibles have returned to normal

Aside from goods, the services balance posted a deficit of US\$3.0bn, sa, in March, modestly lower than the rangebound US\$1.5bn-2.5bn/month over the past one year (Figure 2). It has gradually widened back to pre-pandemic levels (averaging US\$2.2bn/month in 2019), shaped by a fall in freight transport receipts and a rise in net travel imports. The travel deficit was US\$1.4bn in March, gradually increasing since the reopening from US\$0.6bn in 2020-22 and US\$1.0bn in 2023. But it is still marginally lower than the pre-pandemic highs marked in 2017-18, as an increase in tourist inflows provides an offset.

Figure 2: Korea current account breakdown
![](images/7b171aab44a653125a43d15b928ed361e0ed99c2dc39f5c8bb16f12c1536423e.jpg)

The primary income balance marked a US\$2.5bn surplus, sa, in March, following US\$2.2bn in February, rebounding from a temporary US\$0.8bn deficit in January. In the near term, it will see a deficit in non-sa terms in April, due to a seasonal increase in dividend payments to foreigners which are concentrated in April of every year (the April deficits have ranged around US\$3bn-4bn for most of the time since 2015). But in the longer term, the income account should maintain a gradual rising trend in its surplus, as locals have accumulated foreign asset holdings over multiple years.

## Tourist flows rose sharply

Cross-border tourist flows were not fully recovered in 2023 even after the pandemic related travel restrictions were almost eliminated, likely reflecting time lags to restore airline travel capacity. Monthly tourist flows have been rising strongly in recent months, and by March tourist flows had fully recovered to the pre-COVID standard. Inbound travelers rose 113.9%oya and outbound travelers rose 49.1%oya in 1Q24. If seasonally adjusted, inbound traveler flow in March is 21.2% higher than 2019 average, and outbound traveler flow in March is 21.4% higher than pre-COVID 2019 average (Figure 3).

In the balance of payment data, the cross-border traveler flow recovery implies a gradual widening in travel service balance deficits, In the GDP data, the traveler flow recovery would imply greater net service imports and would be negative for overall GDP growth. However some sector level data should be positively affected, including service activities, and private consumption expenditure.

Figure 3: Monthly tourist flows
![](images/25bb27fa7f011aa8cf758f37156498eee6e28253776e33e536f09bb0570a7e6d.jpg)
Source: Korea Tourism Organization and J.P. Morgan

## ASEAN

• Vietnam<sup>’</sup>s IP recovered in April after a quarter of correction, catching up with resilient external demand

SBV started to sell off reserves for VND support, while capping both ends of interest rates through OMO

FX reserves look just about adequate to sustain the intervention, given current account improvement

Vietnam<sup>’</sup>s April data largely aligned with our view for growth normalization after the turbulence of 1Q23<sup>’</sup>s slump, rebound in 2Q-4Q23, and the following correction in 1Q24. We continue to expect that solid exports amid global demand resilience should support growth this year, although downside risks modestly increased from domestic monetary tightening in April. While that didn<sup>’</sup>t lead to sudden liquidity stress, higher interest rates may weigh on investment growth in the near term. As an offset, the latest data showed broadening strength in global growth, which should spill over to Vietnam<sup>’</sup>s exports, and the ongoing tech upcycle could continue to fuel tech exports. In all, we maintain our growth forecast at 6.0%y/y for 2024.

## External demand remains supportive for IP

IP rose 1.3%m/m, sa, in April, narrowing its sequential decline to -0.8%3m/3m, saar, after dipping to -3.2% in March. IP growth should recover further in the near term, as it catches up with resilient external demand. Customs exports growth moderated to 7.6%3m/3m, saar, in April, but as an adjustment from robust 15-20% pace over the past half year (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Vietnam IP and customs exports %3m/3m, saar, both axes
![](images/19910ee97d36f8d5b8edb7658ab5a755867918f46ffefebc5393a2042b82a44c.jpg)

By product, exports of non-tech manufactured goods took a breather after a strong start to the year, marking a slight 2.1%3m/3m, saar, decrease in April. Tech exports also showed some deceleration, but their growth remained resilient at positive 11.1%, implying continued support from the sectoral upcycle (Figure 2).

![](images/3aaf79ce1f119fcaf960fa7104b10e6306c5bb4b38e38e735ab8f1c20663bdc7.jpg)
Source: GSO and J.P. Morgan

By destination, exports to the US slowed to 12.3%3m/3m, saar, in March (latest available) from a 54.5% peak in November, but those to Western Europe (to a 2-year high 55.3%) and China (12.5% after five months of decline) showed improvement to provide an offset (Figure 3), echoing the latest global PMI report that hinted at a narrowing of regional gaps.

![](images/52bdeb0551fcadcfdb47f0c00f58f4e8394ca5ced860be3ccd866bc21e33b666.jpg)

## Sequential disinflation continues

Headline CPI rose 4.4%oya in April, up from 3.4% in January and 4.0% in February-March, mainly due to already-anticipated base effects. In sequential terms, inflation moderated to 2.6%3m/3m, saar, for headline, and stabilized at 2.2% for core (Figure 4). In April, the recent rise in global oil prices started to pass-through to transport CPI (+1.9%m/m), which may continue for a few more months. The stabilization of the heavily weighted food category should provide relief, with regional rice prices stabilizing since the start of the year. The SBV also has a relatively generous inflation target of an annual average of 4%-4.5%, so it should be achieved despite near-term noise, in our view.

Figure 4: Vietnam CPI inflation
![](images/37372e989cdea92fb7801ac0375dadbd7512a790f6fb6d1c8b7ad39b574acb50.jpg)
Source: GSO and J.P. Morgan

## SBV steps up VND support by selling FX

The SBV disclosed that it sold dollars to some banks on April 19, in the face of USDVND touching the upper bound of the trading band (Figure 5). The intervening price was VND 25,450 per USD, which should remain a cap on the pair in the near term, in our opinion, as the central bank revealed its intention to defend this level.

We estimate the SBV held FX reserves of US\$92bn by the end of this March, equivalent to 3.5 months of good imports, which should be just about adequate to sustain the intervention.The central bank<sup>’</sup>s past practices keeping reserves above three months of imports suggests potential room to utilize up to around US\$10bn. It falls short of the US\$5.0bn and US\$ 15.6bn spent back in 2Q and 3Q22, respectively, when the country last saw currency weakness. But a larger current account surplus this time should provide a buffer (US\$-5.0bn in 2Q22, US\$+1.7bn in 3Q22 vs. US\$+7.8bn in 1Q24 in our estimate). Vietnam<sup>’</sup>s BOP has been resilient based on a combination of a steady goods surplus and inward FDI, with an extra contribution from tourism this year (Figure 6).

Figure 5: USDVND exchange rate
![](images/7dafe5825320188e21a443fe4f1d64378bd76c3c2b6d193d6fce9b9f2724ce33.jpg)

Figure 6: Vietnam monthly proxy for BOP
![](images/8780f7a60916614f11a9f7aee2812f18ec8840f20736adf4d6d3d93af861e93c.jpg)

If a dovish pivot from the US Fed in 2H materializes as in our baseline scenario, dollar strength should fade in the near term. For VND, we expect the SBV would prefer to replenish its reserves rather than to allow USDVND to move lower, which should limit the downside of USDVND.

In a risk scenario with an unbearable depletion in FX reserves, the SBV may widen the trading band as it did back in October 2022. If we assume a 1%-pt widening of the band applied to the current reference rate, then USDVND could reach 25,700. However, we assess this is not very likely for now, as SBV must assert its ongoing control over FX movements as far as it can. Thus, not solely relying on FX reverses, the SBV also started a stealth monetary tightening by selling T-bills to set the lower bound to VNIBOR since March and raising their issuance rates from 2.4% on April 1 to 3.75% on April 26. Furthermore, it also raised the reverse repo rate (that serves as the upper bound to VNIBOR) from 4% to 4.25% on April 23 (Figure 7).

Figure 7: Vietnam money market rates and SBV OMO
![](images/6d06600271c2deb04187800b21f90a116e3722c9df168cf7cdd15e121d529a37.jpg)

## Interest rates high, but not so high

That said, the central bank must be also avoiding excessively high interest rates. Credit growth has slowed since the beginning of the year and marked 11.2%oya and 9.9%3m/3m, saar, in March (Figure 8), lower than the annual policy target of 14-15%. Thus, the SBV would like to maintain interest rates high enough to support the local currency but not so high to impede credit growth, juggling between its objectives of FX stability and credit growth facilitation. This intention is proved by its open market operations (OMO) conducted bidirectionally since April (Figure 7), which has constrained both ends of short-term interest rates. It will likely continue to rely on fine-tuning via daily OMO, instead of changing the refinancing rate that would signal a more profound shift in its policy stance.

Figure 8: Vietnam investment and credit growth
![](images/cea145ccbcd3656901d82a4175c59fc083c42d8d3d5735fe9a30955a791f52f1.jpg)
15 16 17 18 19 20Source: SBV and J.P. Morgan. \* GCF growth on an annual basis prior to 2021.

Indonesia<sup>’</sup>s 1Q24 GDP came in as expected, up 5.1% oya and rose 5.1% q/q, saar . Since 2020, net exports have been a solid contributor to growth and look to have been been slowing, in late 2023 and this year with the slowdown in commodityrelated exports.

## India

• March IP expectedly pays back gains

• IP momentum strongly boosted by...

• ...manufacturing exports and public capex

• 1Q24 GDP growth tracking 7%oya

India<sup>’</sup>s March IP printed exactly in line with our expectations, and slightly below consensus at 4.9% oya (J.P. Morgan: 4.9%; consensus: 5.2% oya). On a sequential basis, after three strong prints, IP was expected to deliver some payback in March, and it did, contracting 1.5% m/m, sa in March. Despite the payback, the quarterly annualized momentum of IP remains strong at 14.5% 3m/3m, saar. January-March GDP growth is now tracking 7.0% oya (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Industrial Production (IP)
![](images/2d85ee908239ab1d7034d199adc512757fab94bde1531530f55c30d42caff75b.jpg)

## Exports drive IP momentum

The re-acceleration of IP momentum in recent months reflects both firming manufacturing exports (Figure 2) and that capex spending by both the Center and States has remained strong in the first quarter, bucking expectations of some slowing on the cusp of elections. This is reflected in the fact that the annualized sequential momentum of both capital goods and infrastructure goods production accelerated close to double digits in March (Figures 3 and 4).

Figure 2: Manufacturing exports and Industrial activity
![](images/e70cfd6913041f36b4ab0deccfff8a0a2d6cd96e6b56af113186a058020ecc6e.jpg)

Figure 3: Capital and Infra goods
![](images/244da33e0e6b23975592f6c42b3cb8b336d2b8359b9e80758f55a3dd2efda87c.jpg)

Figure 4: Central Govt. Capex in FY24
![](images/e0cc7cc448f408ce52b80e5da9aa8ea5373cf0191e1f56c0ee4c3597daa36db8.jpg)

In contrast, consumer goods production was softer. Durables output contracted in March (sequentially) but this was not unexpected after three strong months and on the back of auto production contracting that month. Non-durables output disappointed, sharply contracting for a third consecutive month. Rising two-wheeler sales and lower MGNREGA demand have raised hopes of a rural consumption revival which should also translate into firming non-durables output,of which there is no sign just yet.

## Marking up 1Q24 GDP growth

Stronger-than-expected manufacturing exports and domestic public investment has meant that January-March GDP growth is tracking 7% oya. That is stronger than the National Statistics Office estimate of 5.9%. This would push up our FY24 GDP growth forecast to 7.9% oya. We therefore raise our sequential growth forecast in 1Q24, though there is expected to be some payback in the form of lower government spending in 2Q on account of the ongoing general elections.

## Asia Focus: Asset and wealth mgmt business in HK

Hong Kong is an international asset and wealth management (AWM) business center. According to the SFC, the total AUM of the AWM business amounted to HK\$34.7 trillion, or \~12.1x the nominal GDP) in 2021, yet fell to HK\$29.8 trillion in 2022 (or \~10.6x GDP).

The investor base is diversified globally. Assets from non-HK SAR investors represented 64% of the AUM in 2022 (Figure 1), with 23% from North America, 14% from rest of APAC, 11% from Europe and UK, and 9% from mainland China. Compared with 2018, the share of assets from non-mainland China/HK investors was 4%pt higher. Meanwhile, about half of the investments are allocated to mainland China and HK SAR. For the asset management and fund advisory business (by licensed corporations, registered institutions, and insurance companies), 23% of the HK\$11.4 trillion AUM in 2022 are allocated in HK SAR (Figure 2), and 22% are allocated in mainland China, followed by 16% in North America and 14% in Europe and the UK. The share of investment distributed within HK has been falling in the past few years.

Figure 1: HK asset and wealth managment AUM by investor base
![](images/35ae8e43781951016b1eeb74c66156cc3758a057d63b10768f79a827d6a6100e.jpg)
Note: Excl. REITs and assets held under trusts attributable to non-LCs/RIs.

Figure 2: Asset managed in HK by location of investment
![](images/f9f1083259b67729ef51466f852250c277f45b31ca8e3f8fc3b7f3548f95d82c.jpg)
Source: SFC, J.P. Morgan. Note: for asset management and fund advisory business only

The investor base of the private banking and private wealth management business showed a higher level of geographical concentration, with nearly half of the AUM from investors based in HK (Figure 3), and nearly 80% of the AUM from

investors based in mainland China/HK SAR/rest of APAC. For investment allocation, about half of the AUM were invested within the CN/HK/APAC region (Figure 4). Note that the peak of the AUM for PB and PWM business was in 2020, one year earlier than that for the AWM business as a whole.

Figure 3: Hong Kong PB and PWM AUM by investor base
![](images/e7695d1222096f46d02def7c3e4bd348c558dd7dcd202ae85b6b2ef879962724.jpg)
Source: SFC, J.P. Morgan

Figure 4: Hong Kong PB and PWM AUM by location of investment
![](images/f007bf46d539f97a064b5ed77c3fc06ed8b8227f09b6e03237e26eae1c45ad4a.jpg)
Source: SFC, J.P. Morgan

On the other hand, total assets held under trusts have been rising from 2018 to 2022 (Figure 5), and amounted to HK\$5.0 trillion as of end-2022. Around a half of the assets were from mainland China/HK SAR, while the share from North America fell sharply in 2022 to 11% compared with 31% in 2019. Meanwhile, according to the SFC, public funds and pensions funds (including MPF and ORSO) accounted for 70% of the assets held under trusts as in end-2022.

Figure 5: Hong Kong assets under trusts by location of investment
![](images/9d323e3f02b8721f7c0608a6c0be20f1c30cc4a97fccd080dfc2fc9046d40ffd.jpg)
Source: SFC, J.P. Morgan

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer<sup>’</sup>s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer<sup>’</sup>s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its debt obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
